#201. How long will humans be needed for translation?

Google's translation service handles one trillion words per month

"Il pleut des cordes," say the French when it's raining hard.

"It's raining ropes," is the direct translation. It's the kind of idiom which can trip up an AI translator.

"You can imagine the dilemma this throws AI into," says FranÃ§ois Yvon, a research director specialised in automatic translation with the French National Centre for Scientific Research CNRS.

He says a well-trained system will know that ropes are not literally falling from the sky and use the English phrase "it's raining cats and dogs".

"But is it going to find the equivalent when it's translating into Indonesian?" asks Yvon.

A comment on the weather is pretty low-risk, but more and more translation is being done by machines, and on topics with higher stakes than the weather.

In April, Google said people are now translating, external one trillion words a month with Google Translate.

That doesn't count the others: DeepL, Microsoft, Claude, ChatGPT and Gemini, not to mention made-to-measure corporate translation systems.

Direct sales of machine translation, external (i.e. outside general AI subscriptions) were worth an estimated $1.4bn (Â£1bn) last year.

AI translation is the "right call" in many cases says Jonathan Zimmermann

"For plenty of use cases, raw AI translation is now the right call: multiplying your costs by 100 to catch the last few percent of errors isn't rational if you're translating internal documents or product listings," says Jonathan Zimmermann, founder of OKA (Open Knowledge Association), a Swiss non-profit that translates Wikipedia articles into other languages.

"For an encyclopedia it's different. Wikipedia runs on trust. A wrong fact can sit there for years and get cited by journalists and AI systems," he says.

With more than 10,000 published pieces, mostly into Spanish, Portuguese and English, OKA is, Zimmerman says, the single largest contributor of translated articles to the collaborative online encyclopedia.

Which is something it absolutely could not have become without AI.

"Translating 10,000 articles the traditional way would cost millions. In our process, the AI produces the draft, then a human translator checks every sentence," he explains.

But that human input is required less and less.

"The machine output is now good enough that skipping the human entirely has become a serious option, and a real temptation across the industry. Two or three years ago, with tools like DeepL, our translators still rewrote a good chunk of every draft.

"Today the prose often doesn't need fixing at all. So, the question is no longer whether AI translation is good, it clearly is, but whether it's good enough to remove the human," says Zimmermann.

Ariella BaltiÃ© is a translator and legal expert specialised in translating court documents from English into French.

She was contacted recently by someone who'd already translated his documents with AI, asking her to check them.

"There were two major errors right at the beginning," she said.

The AI had translated "Le demandeur" (the plaintiff) as "the requester". "Totally awkward for legal papers," says BaltiÃ©.

When the AI saw "Madame X, Ã©pouse Y" (Ms X, wife of Y'), it came up with "Ms X marries Y".

In other words, it mistook a noun for a verb, completely losing the meaning of the phrase.

The consequences of mistranslation in the legal sphere can be serious. In the US, for example, errors in machine-translated asylum applications have led to delays in the release of detainees.

Ariella BaltiÃ© specialises in legal translation work

Accuracy is a matter of life and death in the medical field.

"The units for dosages and blood tests often include Greek letters," says Carol Bereuter, who is a specialist in medical translations.

She came across one automatically-translated text that had translated the greek letter Î¼ as milli (one thousandth) instead of micro (one millionth).

"That's a serious error. If you give someone a milligram when it's meant to be a microgram, you are giving them a thousand times the dose," points out Bereuter.

She also makes the point that, as AI translators have got better, catching errors has got more difficult.

"Whereas in the past, with things like Google Translate, the sentence would jump out at you because there'd be a grammatical error or it would sound clunky, today it sounds perfectly legitimate. It sounds fluid. It sounds natural. So the errors are a lot harder to spot."

Carol Bereuter is a specialist in medical translations

"If it's something that will bear your signature, there's a risk for your reputation. And if decisions are going to be taken on the basis of a translation concerning a medical procedure, a contract you might sign or a major purchase you might make, the risk is even greater," warns FranÃ§ois Yvon.

Even sentences that seem quite clear to a human can trip up an AI service, he says.

"When you have words with different meanings, it's usually very clear for a human which one applies because they understand the context, but when a machine translator comes across the sentence 'translate into Polish', for example, it is quite capable of deciding it must be all about shoe cream, says Yvon.

Context is often crucial. If an AI translator hasn't grasped that we're talking about a football match it is liable to mis-translate "taking a corner".

Last year OKA ran a study, funded by Wikimedia. It studied 119 articles, 10 language pairs and more than 1,000 hours of translator work.

"27% of the AI-generated text still got corrected before publication. Three quarters of those corrections were errors the AI introduced, and 5 to 6% of errors changed the meaning of the original text," says Zimmerman.

Eradicating those errors is likely to be challenging.

"As things stand, I think the fundamental way these systems function makes it quite hard to imagine how they could become 100% reliable," says FranÃ§ois Yvon.

This is because probability is a crucial part of the way AI translates each word rather than another and what is probably correct is not the same as what is correct. Especially in the realm of something as subtle and surprising as language.

He thinks a new generation of tech will be an improvement.

"We will see translation systems that are increasingly capable of evaluating what they do and correcting themselves using different AI tools simultaneously: one to do the translation, a second to criticize it and a third to produce a new version, for example."

But perhaps the big moment will be when AI can show doubt.

"The real breakthrough won't come until these systems are not merely capable of translating but also of telling us when they're not sure," Yvon says.

#200. Yum Brands earnings beat estimates, but cyclospora outbreak weighs on Taco Bell

Yum Brands on Tuesday reported earnings that beat expectations, but a cyclospora outbreak linked to iceberg lettuce at Taco Bell weighed on the company's same-store sales growth.

However, Yum said Taco Bell, which the company considers a "growth engine" for its business, has since rebounded.

"Since the end of September, Taco Bell U.S. has been outpacing the [quick-service restaurant] industry on same-store sales growth," Yum CEO Chris Turner said in a statement.

Taco Bell's same-store sales are up about 1% so far in the fourth quarter, CFO Ranjith Roy said on the company's earnings conference call Tuesday. In the last four weeks ended Friday, same-store sales have risen 3% compared with the year-ago period.

Here's what the company reported compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Yum reported fiscal third-quarter net income of $1.62 billion, or $5.92 per share, up from $397 million, or $1.41 per share, a year earlier.

Excluding gains from its divestiture of Pizza Hut and other items, the restaurant company earned $1.56 per share.

During the quarter, Yum completed its divestiture of Pizza Hut. It closed the sale of the pizza chain's business in mainland China to Yum China on Aug. 7 and sold the rest of Pizza Hut to LongRange Capital on Sept. 1. The company's third-quarter results include Pizza Hut up until those dates.

Excluding Pizza Hut, Yum's same-store sales were flat for the quarter as Taco Bell weighed on the company's overall results. Taco Bell's same-store sales declined by 4% as diners temporarily pulled back on visits due to the foodborne illness outbreak.

Yum executives previously said the weekend of July 18 saw the most severe impact to sales, but trends have improved since then.

Taco Bell's most loyal customers returned faster, Yum said, thanks in part to weekly Tuesday deals for loyalty program members after the cyclospora outbreak took over headlines.

Traffic from "medium" and "light" customers has bounced back to about 95% of prior levels, while visits from "heavy" users is back to normal, Roy said. To attract more casual diners, Taco Bell has been relying on star power, like a collaboration with pop star Olivia Rodrigo on a pink-colored Mountain Dew Baja Blast.

Outside of the U.S., Taco Bell's much smaller international business reported same-store sales growth of 5%.

KFC's same-store sales ticked up 1%, helped by system sales growth in China and Europe. The U.S., now the chicken chain's third-largest market, saw system sales shrink 6% in the quarter.

Like Restaurant Brands International's Popeyes, KFC has struggled in the U.S. amid a wave of new competitors and more budget-conscious consumers. Its home market represents only about 5% of KFC's operating profit, but it remains a symbolically important part of the business for Yum investors.

During the quarter, KFC U.S. launched popcorn chicken, which sold out in many restaurants. However, Yum said the limited-time menu item was not enough to offset the successful Hot & Spicy Wings and Potato Wedges promotion during the year-ago period.

Globally, KFC is trying to become "the most modern chicken QSR," Turner said on the company's earnings conference call. To achieve that lofty goal, he said the chain will roll out buzzy promotions, a new brand identity, and a wider menu that includes upgraded sauces and more beverages.

#199. Pfizer hikes full-year outlook on new product growth, taps company veteran as new CFO

Pfizer on Tuesday reported fiscal third-quarter results that topped estimates and hiked its full-year outlook, as demand for its newly launched and acquired products grows.

Pfizer also Tuesday announced that Cecile Guegan, who has held various finance leadership roles at the company over the last two decades, stepped in as chief financial officer this month. She had served as interim CFO since August, when Pfizer's former CFO, Dave Denton, stepped down to take the same position at Nike.

The company is now expecting full-year revenue to total $62 billion to $64 billion, which compares with a previous outlook of $60.5 billion to $62.5 billion. The low end of that sales range would be roughly flat or down slightly compared with 2025 revenue of $62.6 billion. Still, Pfizer cited its "strong year-to-date performance" as a driver for the guidance hike.

In an exclusive interview with CNBC, Pfizer CEO Albert Bourla said the company's Covid product revenue was "very low" this quarter and year, but called it "positive news for us."

"Covid revenues was an overhang on the performance of our stock because of the perception that it is volatile and it's material to us. Actually, we've proven that we can make it irrelevant," Bourla said. "If anything, only an upside because, in the lowest possible Covid performance, we are beating, and we are raising by $1.5 billion because of the overperformance of the other products."

Pfizer reaffirmed its Covid product outlook of roughly $4 billion for the year, expecting the majority of Covid shot sales in the fourth quarter during vaccination season, Guegan said during the call.

The pharmaceutical giant now expects full-year adjusted profit of between $2.95 and $3.05 per share. That compares with previous guidance of between $2.80 and $3 per share.

That includes a one-time $650 million charge, or a negative impact of 10 cents per share, recorded in the third quarter tied to the company's licensing and collaboration agreement with Chinese cancer drugmaker Innovent Biologics.

Pfizer is working on a comeback story after its massive pandemic-era success waned. As demand for Covid products drops and generic rivals threaten its biggest moneymakers, Bourla is looking to jump-start growth through strategic acquisitions, fresh cancer drugs and a big bet on weight loss treatments through its acquisition of Metsera.

"In addition to the revenues that they generate now, and they will continue generating so that we can overcome the patent, the loss of patent exclusivity, we are bringing pipeline that will perform even better in terms of growth," Bourla told CNBC, referring to Pfizer's launched and acquired products.

Pfizer in August expanded its multiyear cost-cutting efforts to help offset sharp declines in Covid product sales, and is now targeting roughly $9.7 billion in net savings through 2029.

Here's what the company reported for the third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Pfizer reported third-quarter revenue of $16.58 billion, relatively flat from the same period a year ago. The company cited an operational decrease of $57 million, primarily due to lower Covid revenues, and a $15 million negative impact from foreign exchange.

That was offset by strong sales of key products like its blood thinner Eliquis and targeted cancer drug Padcev, which jumped and beat expectations.

Eliquis in particular raked in $2.52 billion in sales, up 25%. Analysts were expecting revenue of $2.40 billion, according to StreetAccount. Padcev posted $720 million in revenue for the period, which compares with the $658 million that analysts were expecting.

Sales of Pfizer's newly launched and acquired products contributed $3.8 billion, growing 32% operationally, Guegan said during the earnings call. That includes Padcev, its respiratory syncytial virus vaccine Abrysvo, its pneumococcal shot Prevnar 20 and oral lung cancer drug Lorbrena, among several other products.

'We continue to invest behind this group of products to mitigate [loss of exclusivity] headwinds over the next several years," Guegan said. "In addition, this quarter's performance demonstrates clear progress against our objective of returning to growth toward the end of the decade."

Pfizer's Covid products also topped estimates for the quarter despite seeing steep sales declines. The company's Covid vaccine generated $808 million in sales, which compares with the $770.4 million that analysts were expecting, StreetAccount estimates show. Meanwhile, Pfizer's antiviral pill, Paxlovid, booked $449 million in sales, coming in above the $394.1 million that analysts were expecting.

The company booked net income of $3.24 billion, or 56 cents per share, for the period. That compares with net income of $3.54 billion, or 62 cents per share, during the third quarter of 2025.

Excluding certain items, including restructuring charges and costs associated with intangible assets, Pfizer posted earnings per share of 87 cents for the quarter.

#197. As Christmas rush looms, how do China-made toys find their way to the US?

China is the world's largest toymaker, producing the majority of toys on sale in the US

On the underside of most children's toys on sale in the US you'll find the same three words â "Made in China".

Nearly eight in 10 toys (78.3%) imported into the country in 2024 came from China, according to US government figures. , external

Last year, toys imported from China were worth $9.8bn (Â£7.4bn), trade figures show. , external

With Christmas on the horizon, the toy industry in the US and elsewhere is about to have its busiest time of the year by far.

As retailers prepare their stocks for the festive rush, be they online giants or "mom-and-pop" physical stores, the BBC World Service's Follow the Money series traces how a toy gets from a factory in China to the US. And where the money goes.

Guangdong province, which neighbours Hong Kong in southern China, is the heart of the country's toy manufacturing industry.

In the city of Dongguan workers on the floor of the Mihox factory pop plastic toys out of steel moulds, and pile them into crates ready for painting.

The company says it employs more than 800 people and makes around 55 million toys a year for the American market, which represents half of its business.

Follow the money: The journey of a $30 toy from China to America

Every toy starts with a design from a customer, such as an American toy company. The factory then makes a prototype, and builds moulds from which plastic parts and other components are formed.

Going from the first idea to finished toy takes one to two years, says Melanie Wang from the Mihox sales team. Much of that is the customer going back to the drawing board. "They modified the design again, again, and again to improve it."

Once the design is signed off, things move more quickly. A mould takes about a month to make, and the production run lasts about three months.

The Mihox factory in Dongguan employs more than 800 people

So, how much of the US retail price does the factory get? "About 20%," says Wang.

On a $30 toy, that's $6, which has to cover the price of the plastic and other materials, production costs including wages, and what profit the factory makes.

Chinese factories typically make a profit of between 3% and 10%, according to Jay Foreman, chief executive of US toy firm Basic Fun. So for a $30 toy that's between 60 and 18 cents of the $6 the factory charges to make it.

Samson Ko is president of the Toys Manufacturers' Association of Hong Kong. Many of his members moved their factories across the border into mainland China in the 1980s, attracted by cheaper land and labour.

He says the way toys are made has barely changed since then. "Automation now takes place mostly in the moulding and the packaging process. But for soft toys, or even some of the electronics nowadays, it's still very labour intensive."

And Chinese labour isn't cheap anymore, he adds, after recent rises.

Melanie Wang explains that Mihox gets 20% of a toy's retail price

Average pay in Vietnam is about half of that in China, said one report, external at the start of this year.

Yet fewer than 10% of Ko's members have moved any production outside China, and in those cases mostly only because customers asked them to do so.

The Chinese factories remain busy, he says, because a single toy can undergo more than one manufacturing process, from moulding the parts, to adding electronics, sewing materials, and final assembly. This often requires it travelling to different factories.

Ko adds that in Guangdong these separate facilities are located close to each other, and used to "working in harmony". He says this makes the whole manufacturing process run more smoothly than in other countries.

From the factory, the toy goes by truck to a port, such as Shenzhen, the biggest in Guangdong.

Ivy Tse is co-founder of FreightAmigo, a Hong Kong logistics company that runs an online platform for both Hong Kong and mainland China exporters. These businesses can book a cargo container, sea passage to an overseas destination, arrange insurance cover, and secure finance.

Tse says it takes about 15 days for a cargo ship to get from Shenzhen to the US west coast, and 25 to 30 days to the east coast. This year's Christmas toys left China in August and September.

Freight, from factory to the US, usually adds about 10% to the manufacturing cost of a toy, adds Tse. So if we stick to a toy that retails for $30 in the US, and therefore costs $6 to make in China, the cost of getting it to the states is 60 cents.

Once the toy reaches the US there is currently a tariff on Chinese toys that has to be paid to the US Customs and Border Protection (CBP).

Following talks between President Trump and Chinese leader Xi Jinping last month, the two governments have recommended toys for lower tariffs. But no new rate or start date has been set, and the US must first hold a public consultation. For now the basic rate is 12.5%, and some toys pay more.

This is charged on the manufacturing price of the toy, not on how much it retails for. So for a $30 toy, that basic rate is 12.5% of $6, which is 75 cents.

This tariff is paid for by the American company that has imported the toys. One such firm is Learning Resources, a family business based near Chicago that sells educational toys. Half of these it imports from China.

"We pay 100% of the tariffs, let there be no doubt about that," says the company's chief executive Rick Woldenberg.

Jay Foreman says that most of the money spent on a toy in the US doesn't leave the country

The cost of making the $30 toy, getting it to the US, and paying the import tariff, comes to $7.35. So that leaves $22.65, or 75.5% of the total.

That money never leaves the US, explains Foreman from Basic Fun. His firm, which is based in Florida, makes Tonka trucks and Care Bears cuddly toys.

"It's kept by my company, the [US] transportation company, the retailer," he says.

If we allow $2.65 for the US delivery firm, the toy company and the retailer will typically share the remaining revenues 50-50. So that would give them $10 each.

While profit margins are commercially sensitive and therefore not generally revealed, industry studies show that the toy firm and retailer would usually make a profit of up to 25% or $2.50.

Getting a toy onto the shelves in the US requires both a lot of work and a long journey.

#195. Living with a stink? Technology might help

The operators of Newby Island landfill have been ordered to take action over odours

Milpitas, an affluent city in California's Silicon Valley tech hub, has an odour problem.

For decades, residents have had to endure rank smells wafting from industrial sites, among them a 342-acre landfill.

"When I first moved here, I could not open my window for over 10 years," says Chia-Ling Kong, an engineer for a tech firm who lives around half a mile from the dump.

While things have got better in recent years, Kong says she and her neighbours must still contend with around "two or three hours of bad smells a day".

Perhaps befitting Silicon Valley, the community is putting its hopes in technology.

The operators of Newby Island landfill have been ordered by local authorities to use drones and install monitors to systematically track and measure the chemicals responsible for bad odours. They have also been told to automate the collection of landfill gases responsible for smells.

Kong is optimistic a data-driven approach will make a difference. "Hopefully with this continuous monitoring, it makes it easier to pinpoint problems at the moment it starts."

The operators of the landfill say they are committed to operating it safely and protecting air quality.

Just like in Milpitas, where Kong is part of a citizen's campaign against the odours, communities around the world are raising a stink about bad smells making day-to-day life miserable. Sometimes a rubbish dump is to blame, or it might be a sewage treatment works, or a reeking factory.

When the complaints start stacking up, resolving them is not always simple. Many countries have no regulations in place dictating how to manage smells.

In the US, for example, the Environmental Protection Agency does not regulate environmental odours as a pollutant. This leaves states and local governments to take charge of the issue.

Urban sprawl is often part of the problem, bringing communities closer to stinky facilities that may historically have been kept at a distance.

This is creating more demand for technologies to monitor and prevent smells. Odour consultant Micheal Bull says people are increasingly concerned about neighbourhood stinks.

"You're getting developments being built nearer to odorous processesâ¦ as land has become more valuable, there is also infilling," Bull says. He is speaking about his experiences in the UK, but it's a similar story in many parts of the world.

Bad smells from waste sites are among the most difficult to resolve

The technologies used to tackle industrial pongs generally fall into three categories: they absorb odours; convert them into something less smelly through a chemical reaction; or burn them away.

Experts on fixing industrial odours say that the best technologies, when used correctly, can remove 90% or more of the target compounds responsible for smells.

But this can be expensive and some operators opt for simpler and cheaper solutions. These include masking odours from landfill or sewage processing by spraying giant quantities of deodorisers.

Odour experts contacted for this article were dubious about the effectiveness of such industrial scale air fresheners. "In my view they're not likely to change the odours," says Bull, who likens them to fabric conditioner.

Bad smells from waste sites â a common cause of complaints â are among the stinkiest and most difficult to resolve, according to Carlos Diaz, an environmental consultant based in Spain. "Landfill is difficult to treat because you have a very large surface," he says.

In some places at least, technology is making a real difference. Residents in Des Moines, Iowa, have long complained of the so-called Eastside Funk, a putrid scent that residents report drifts over parts of the city.

A study commissioned by city authorities showed that a trio of industrial meat companies were among culprits.

"Within 10 miles, there's about half a million people," says Dalton Jacobus, who works in the Department of Neighborhood Services. "If the winds are out of the wrong direction, 100,000 people can be in the middle of that odour plume."

The authorities are now paying around $240,000 (Â£182,000) a year on technology to track the stink, so they can take action. "What we wanted to do was build a ring of odour monitors around the facilities that we suspected of emitting odours," says Jacobus.

The monitors, which measure levels of chemical compounds in the air, work in combination with a weather station to provide real-time data on the concentrations that are coming from each facility based off the wind patterns.

This has put an end to finger pointing about who's to blame each time a smell sparks complaints, says Jacobus. "Now we can say with reasonable certainty that 'this odour event did originate from your facility, what was going on'?"

In Des Moines, authorities have set up "electronic noses" to pinpoint bad smells

Meanwhile, some of the sites that have been blamed for causing unpleasant smells in Des Moines have spent millions of dollars to upgrade their own odour control systems.

Smithfield Foods, which operates a meat processing in Des Moines, says it has invested $2m to install a regenerative thermal oxidiser that destroys the chemicals associated with bad smells. Even so, the company says it does not believe it is the source of odour complaints.

Before installing the monitors â which some local companies help pay for â officials in Des Moines would drive out to the source of a complaint to sniff the air.

Relying on the human nose to sniff out industrial smells is still commonplace. Claudia Barrios ObregÃ³n from Kunak, the company behind the monitors in Des Moines, says: "Right now, the way that cities control or measure odours is olfactometry: a human panel measures and gives an opinion of what the odour is like. But that's not that realistic."

The Spanish company's monitors are an example of so-called electronic noses that make it easier to detect smells.

Whatever technology is used, it can be hard to keep everyone happy, as smells are so subjective. "Unfortunately, the general rule is that the nastier we perceive the odour to be, the more sensitive towards it we are," says Johan LundstrÃ¶m, a professor in the science of smell at the Karolinska Institute in Stockholm, Sweden.

The extreme engineering of aircraft windows

#193. Elon Musk’s record election bet backfired. The backlash may reach beyond one billionaire

Democrats' sweeping midterms victory marked a major rejection of Elon Musk and the artificial-intelligence industry, as a broad backlash against U.S. President Donald Trump and his tech allies put their political influence — and the debt-fueled data center buildout — under sharper scrutiny.

Musk, who spent a record of at least $474 million on the midterms, funneled $224 million, or nearly half, into Texas alone, according to a CNBC analysis of campaign finance records, only to see his favored candidates, Republican Senate nominee Ken Paxton and Gov. Greg Abbott, trounced in a Democratic wave.

The defeats rattled markets overnight, sending SpaceX and Tesla shares lower, both of which have long flourished under Abbott's business-friendly policies. Meanwhile, broader tech stocks came under pressure as Democrats secured control of Congress.

Across the country, voters pushed back against billionaire influence and the growing power of technology companies, electing critics of the AI industry and approving restrictions on data centers fueling its expansion.

"We must get big money out of our politics, so no billionaire or trillionaire can buy our elections," Democratic Senator-elect James Talarico said in his victory speech, without naming Musk. "We must unrig this economic system that only benefits the top 1%."

J.C. Lintzenich, a visiting senior fellow at the German Marshall Fund, pointed to Musk's massive spending in Texas, telling CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Wednesday that the results were "clearly a rejection of the Elon Musk aspect that has found its way into the Trump orbit."

Texas has grown to be homebase for much of Musk's Empire. Tesla operates its largest U.S. vehicle factory outside Austin, SpaceX launches rockets from its Starbase complex in South Texas, and Musk is pursuing additional investments in AI and semiconductor manufacturing.

These operations rely heavily on state decisions regarding electricity, water, environmental permits, infrastructure, and tax incentives. Democratic Governor-elect Gina Hinojosa — who campaigned against generous corporate subsidies, and Musk himself — will wield significant influence over those decisions.

Hinojosa has called for a moratorium on new data centers, an end to industry tax breaks and requirements that developers cover their full electricity costs. In her victory speech Tuesday, she vowed to take power from the "powerful people who have gotten very comfortable running this state."

Republicans still control the Legislature, limiting Hinojosa's ability to overhaul state policy. But her administration could complicate Musk's expansion plans with a tougher approach to permitting and business incentives.

Nationwide, Musk's America PAC spent nearly $87 million across 44 federal races, according to a CNBC analysis of FEC filings. By Wednesday morning, Republicans it backed had lost 29 contests and won just three, with 12 still uncalled. More than $80 million — or 93% of its spending — went toward races Republicans lost.

The outcome leaves Musk with fewer allies in a Democratic-controlled Congress that could investigate his federal contracts, his past leadership of Trump's Department of Government Efficiency and his companies' ties to Washington.

Congressional scrutiny may slow SpaceX's lucrative and fast-growing government contracts, but Trump still controls executive agencies and holds veto power, limiting Democrats' ability to reverse policies that benefit Musk's defense-tech businesses.

The defeats aren't the first time that Musk's political spending has failed to produce his desired result.

America PAC backed eight losing congressional candidates in 2024, and last year Musk spent over $20 million trying to elect a conservative in Wisconsin. Though Musk promised last year to spend "a lot less" on politics, he ultimately became the biggest known donor in the 2026 midterms.

Longtime Tesla investor Ron Baron warned in March 2025 that Musk's political activism risked driving away customers, telling CNBC: "I don't think it's a great thing to alienate half the population."

Others cheered Musk's close relationship with Trump, and his influence over everything from regulation to Pentagon tech spending.

Musk was not the only tech figure who spent big.

OpenAI President Greg Brockman and his wife, Anna, contributed $25 million to Leading the Future, a political operation seeking to shape AI regulation, according to FEC filings. Venture capital firm Andreessen Horowitz contributed another $50 million.

While the industry secured some victories – such as defeating New York lawmaker Alex Bores, an advocate for tougher AI safeguards, in a Democratic congressional primary – winning over local communities proved far more difficult.

In Ohio, Democrat Sherrod Brown defeated Republican Sen. Jon Husted after attacking his support for data centers. Democrats spent nearly $28 million on advertisements criticizing data centers, according to AdImpact.

In South Carolina, Democrat Annie Andrews defeated Republican Sen. Darline Graham despite a late advertising push by Building the Future, a new data center industry super PAC that made Graham its first candidate to back. The group reserved $1.5 million in ads supporting Graham, while Andrews campaigned for a two-year moratorium on new data centers.

Nationwide, campaigns and outside groups spent $173 million on ads about data centers during the 2026 general election, with the ads almost entirely negative, according to AdImpact. Democratic advertisers accounted for $140 million of that spending. Voters also considered 41 local data center measures Tuesday, with at least 10 communities approving bans or restrictions.

The financial stakes are enormously high.

JPMorgan Asset Management estimates the AI infrastructure buildout could cost roughly $5 trillion through 2030, including about $2 trillion financed through investment-grade debt markets.

Projects cannot generate anticipated returns until fully operational. Permitting delays can postpone completion and revenue, while higher utility and borrowing costs can raise project expenses and make expected returns harder to achieve.

#191. The tech helping crack decades-old cold cases

Crime scene evidence is often not enough to identify suspects

Randy Gail Sperino was last seen alive on the evening of 9 November 1993, as she was getting into a pickup truck in Granite City, Illinois.

The 34-year-old's body was found hours later in a field nearby, the victim of blunt force trauma.

The identity of her killer was a mystery. DNA evidence found at the crime scene was tested against standard law enforcement databases, but no matches were found.

For three decades, investigators from the Madison County Sheriff's Office chased leads and interviewed hundreds of people as they attempted to identify the perpetrator.

The case looked like it would remain one of more than 250,000 unsolved homicides in the US.

Then, last year, detectives tried a fresh approach. They sent genetic evidence to Othram, a Houston-based company that uses DNA technology to help identify the perpetrators in cold and current crime cases.

The Madison County detectives had been inspired by investigative genetic genealogy techniques used to identify the Golden State Killer, Joseph James DeAngelo, back in 2018. In that case, an investigative team constructed a family tree to help identify the killer.

They were able to do so thanks to DNA data that relatives had provided to commercial databases, similar to consumer ancestry platforms.

Unlike traditional forensic DNA testing, which generally relies on a direct comparison between evidence and a known person, investigative genetic genealogy can identify distant genetic relatives of an unknown individual.

Othram set about applying this technique to track down Sperino's killer. While the suspect's own DNA profile wasn't available, profiles submitted by his extended relatives allowed the company to identify a male family member.

"That lead broke the case open," said Captain Tim Lawrence of Madison County Sheriff's Office, which was responsible for investigating the crime.

It was a crucial clue that led the detectives to Albert "Buddy" Zigler, 70, who police say then confessed to the murder. He was charged in May this year.

Wes Sperino, Gail's son, said at a press conference: "I finally get closure and I finally know who did this."

Albert Zigler was charged with murder in May

The technique that led detectives to Zigler is credited with revolutionising how forensic cases are solved.

It has been highly successful in helping solve cases involving unidentified human remains and identifying suspects in longstanding investigations.

Othram claims it has helped solve thousands of cases. The company's chief executive David Mittelman likens investigative genetic genealogy to a GPS system: "The DNA profile is the GPS signal and it says 'you belong here.' But to understand where 'here' is, you need a map."

Public records, obituaries, and census data build that map so Othram's scientists can establish how different family trees connect.

However, Mittelman is quick to point out that DNA technology can only go so far before "a cascade of investigative steps" are needed.

An Othram scientist prepares a bone for DNA extraction

DNA science isn't the only technology that is helping detectives solve cold cases.

One of the challenges facing detectives on major investigations like the hunt for Sperino's attacker is the sheer amount of evidence they dig up.

Officers investigating her murder realised AI could help them wade through the mountains of evidence they had accumulated since 1993.

They used a platform called Centerline AI, from Florida software company CentralSquare, to analyse more than 2,000 pages of records and identify evidence for the DNA testing that helped to break the case.

The AI helped them reconstruct timelines and organise witness information. "It's so much easier than having a detective have to read through 2,000 pages," Lawrence says. His team could interact with the AI like a chat bot.

This also helped when it came to interviewing Zigler after they had tracked him down. Detectives were able to ask questions of the AI to help "figure out if Zigler was being truthful", says Lawrence.

But the technology is "only as good as the investigator," says the captain. There comes a point, he says, "where you leave the technology behind and you have to go track people down and talk to them."

Investigative genetic genealogy has its limitations too. Mittelman says: "It can't solve every case. If you have no DNA, you can't really use this technique."

"But this technique is a lot more powerful and a lot more likely to yield identities than having to rely on the chance that you find a match somewhere in a database," he adds.

While firms like Othram only use ancestry platforms where users have provided consent for law enforcement matching, there remain some concerns about the ethical implications of using this data.

A lack of funding is another hurdle. Mittelman says: "Every law enforcement agency wants to do it. There's just not a lot of funding for it."

Sometimes cases are funded through donations or crowdfunding. Othram operates a website listing cases that members of the public can help fund.

While investigative genetic genealogy has been adopted in countries such as Sweden, Denmark and Australia, it is not yet used in the UK. The Home Office is conducting a study to consider whether it should be adopted in the UK but did not respond to a request for comment.

The UK is already in a strong position, says Syndercombe Court. "We have such an efficient DNA database that if you can get any DNA out of a crime scene, you've got over a 65% chance of putting a name to that person, which is enormously powerful," she says.

#189. SpaceX signs deal with Qualcomm to provide network technology for U.S. mobile service

SpaceX announced a deal with chipmaker Qualcomm to provide key network technology for Elon Musk's Starlink Mobile service, a challenger to Verizon, AT&T and T-Mobile.

Qualcomm said on Wednesday that the terrestrial network it will help SpaceX build will "complement Starlink's satellite capabilities," with a planned rollout to begin next year. Financial terms of the multiyear deal weren't disclosed.

"Together with SpaceX, we are building world-leading cellular infrastructure in the United States, advancing 5G today, with an opportunity to lay the foundation for AI-native 6G in the years ahead," Durga Malladi, an executive vice president at Qualcomm, said in the statement.

Starlink is a critical piece of SpaceX's business, accounting for 36% of the company's revenue in the third quarter and 85% of its operating profit. But to date, the company's consumer business has been limited when it comes to mobile direct-to-device services as its satellite network can't provide all the voice, video and data coverage that competitors offer on popular smartphones.

Now SpaceX is snapping up spectrum and forging partnerships to take on the major mobile carriers. Last month, the company announced an agreement to purchase a nationwide spectrum portfolio in the 800 MHz band. The news sent shares of U.S. carriers reeling. Earlier this week, the Washington Post reported that SpaceX is in talks to acquire EchoStar's Boost Mobile.

SpaceX went public in June in the largest IPO on record and is now valued at close to $2 trillion.

With Qualcomm, SpaceX will be using the chipmaker's Dragonwing cellular infrastructure hardware and software in base stations to enable high-capacity coverage for Starlink Mobile.

Qualcomm has a long history of making chips for cellular networks, but is best known for making the modems and radios that go inside devices, not base stations for running a wireless network. The company has targeted carriers and other infrastructure companies since it made a bigger push into the 5G infrastructure market in 2020.

SpaceX represents one of Qualcomm's highest-profile customers or partners in 5G infrastructure, following earlier deals with Verizon, Dish, and Europe's Vodafone.

While SpaceX plans to use Qualcomm's technology to establish tens of thousands of new cell sites, it hasn't announced partners among cell tower companies or said how many new towers may be required.

Mobile carriers generally lease space from companies such as American Tower and Crown Castle, which commonly host multiple carriers from one site.

SpaceX did not respond to CNBC's request for additional information.

-- CNBC's Kif Leswing contributed reporting.

#187. The grads ditching the UK for a fresh start abroad

Charlie West, left, Rachel James and Owein Iveson have all moved overseas

Last year Rachel James quit her corporate job in London, packed a suitcase and booked a one-way ticket to Sydney, without a job lined up or a plan in place.

"I went for a fresh start," says the 26-year-old from Surrey. "To take a risk and try something completely different. With the worst case being there will always be a flight home if all fails."

More than one in 10 UK university students are now planning to move abroad for better opportunities after they have graduated, up from 7.8% two years ago, according to a survey, external of 15,000 final year university students.

Meanwhile, a study by the Institute of Student Employers (ISE) says that competition for graduate jobs in the UK was at a three-decade high.

UK employers now receive 140 applications per graduate vacancy on average, an increase of 14% from 2025, and up from just 30 applications per vacancy 20 years ago, according to the ISE's report, external.

For young British adults going abroad, where are they heading?

While there is no age-specific data for which countries people go to, Australia remains by far the most popular nation for British adults overall who move abroad, official figures show., external

In addition to Australia's weather and use of the English language, another reason is its visa-friendly policy. Under its Working Holiday Maker programme,, external British passport holders between the ages of 18 and 35 can live in Australia for up to three years.

Rachel James says that Sydney is a nicer place to live and work than London

Rachel, who graduated from Loughborough University in 2022 with a degree in English, had been working for accountancy giant PricewaterhouseCoopers in the UK. She now has a job in marketing in Sydney for gym chain Fitness First.

She says her annual salary has gone down from Â£42,000 to Â£37,000, while she now pays $AUS 2,000 (Â£1,000) a month to rent a two-bedroom apartment with a friend, similar to what she was paying in London.

Despite having less disposable income, she has no regrets. "The weather's beautiful, even in the winter, and the walks on my doorstep are unmatched.

"People want to leave work at 5pm so they can go and do their hobbies - whether it's surfing, running, enjoying the sunset, a picnic or sunset yoga. I didn't come with career progression as a focus - it's more about the different way of life."

Around two-thirds of British nationals who emigrate are aged between 16 and 34, according to the most recent figures, external from the UK's Office for National Statistics.

It said that 75,000 more people in that age bracket left the UK in 2025 than arrived.

After Australia the next most popular destination for British adults is the US.

Charlie West says that working in the US has given him better pay and more opportunities

Charlie West, 25, from Cheshire, moved to the States in 2024 for better pay and opportunities in academia after he completed a masters in medieval studies at Oxford University.

West is now two years in to a six-year funded PhD course at Yale's Department of Italian Studies, which includes him teaching.

"I really wanted to do a PhD but it's difficult to get funding in the UK, particularly in the humanities, and funded stipends are about Â£21,000. I'd have had to work [outside academia] at the same time to afford to live.

"In the US I'm paid $51,000 a year (Â£37,770), about to rise to $52,000, and it comes with a lot more opportunities to teach and travel to conferences."

The European Union also remains popular with young Britons looking to move abroad, although the visa requirements since Brexit have made it more difficult.

One way to get into the European jobs market is to have studied in the EU. International graduates can apply for a permanent residence permit after their degree ends.

Owein Iveson has no plans to return to the UK from Finland

This is what Owein Iveson, 24, from Sunderland, is doing as he is about to graduate from Finland's Aalto University with a masters in design.

Owein says he "fell in love with the country" after visiting for the first time in 2020.

"Finland's a beautiful country," he says. "The streets are very clean. It's a very quiet lifestyle. The nature is fantastic and the public transport works really efficiently."

Owein lives in Espoo, a city about 15 minutes on the metro from Helsinki, and doesn't expect to return to the UK anytime soon.

"To be honest the jobs market is not great in Finland, but there is high demand for IT workers, and if you can work across the EU there are many more opportunities."

Immigration lawyer John Kiely, a London-based partner at international law firm Howard Kennedy, says it is easy to see why more UK graduates are looking overseas.

"When you combine student debt, high housing costs, relatively weak wage growth and uncertainty about future employment, the willingness to roll the dice somewhere else becomes quite rational."

Back in Sydney, Rachel says the city simply offers a better quality of life than London.

"I don't see life in Sydney as any more expensive than if I was in London, but personally I have found the perks in Sydney better."

#185. The Indian workers filming their work to train AI

Mohammed Ashim is paid to film his hands at work

Mohammed Ashim has been cutting hair and trimming beards for four years in Bengaluru.

But recently the 22-year old has been strapping a camera to his head to record his work.

The camera only records his hands and fingers - his client's faces are blurred out.

"The routine is simple, but you have to be disciplined about how you move. The operations team taught me that my hands always have to stay cleanly inside the frame, the lighting has to be sharp, and I can't tilt my head too abruptly while working."

He wears the camera for five or six hours a day.

"It's good money, but honestly, the feeling of doing something this futuristic is what keeps me excited. I never imagined my razor techniques would be useful to a global tech company," says Ashim.

He is one of hundreds of thousands of workers across the world paid to record their activities.

Ashim's footage is collected by a local company, which might have hundreds of people filming their work.

That video is then sold on to technology companies and research organisations who use it to train artificial intelligence - in particular so-called physical AI, a type of artificial intelligence that can control robots in the real world.

Rashdan Technologies collects and processes such human generated data.

"Every robotics company, every frontier AI lab, has a specific kind of data they want: industrial tasks, agriculture, household chores, small business work like pottery, garment-making, embroidery, candle-making. We go wherever the task is," says co-founder and CEO Shahil Baig.

His company pays $5 to $10 (Â£3.74; Â£7.47) for eight to 10 hours of recorded work.

"For a lot of these people, that's meaningful," Baig says.

Making sure workers give their consent and are comfortable with the process is important, he says.

"We record hands, not faces, and where multi-camera rigs do capture more of the body or environment, we blur faces out. No names, no contact details, nothing that identifies them personally goes into what we hand off."

But his customers are getting more demanding.

"Now clients are asking for four to six cameras rigged around a person's head to cover the entire scene, not just the hands.

"Getting someone to agree to one small camera is one conversation. Getting them to agree to wearing what's effectively a five-camera headset is a completely different, and harder, one. We pay more for that tier of data collection because of what we're asking of people."

And the industry is becoming more crowded, Agarwal expects dozens more competitors to emerge in India this year.

Shahil Baig expects dozens more firms to start collecting video of workers

If you want robots to navigate the real world, they can't just be trained inside computer simulations, argues Manish Agarwal, the founder of Humyn Labs, which collects and processes human-generated data.

Most of that data comes from video, but sometimes collectors wear hardware on their hands and fingers to capture physical contact.

"Physical tasks require high-quality signals involving limb movements, fingers, wrists, elbows, shoulders and dynamic force application. For example: applying the correct grip force so a plastic bottle doesn't crumble versus a steel glass," he says.

And until recently, that data was not available.

"There is no existing internet archive of embodied human activity - no dataset of a kitchen being cleaned, or a car being assembled by hands that know exactly how much force to apply. That data has to be captured from scratch, in the real world, across diverse environments," says Agarwal, whose company has bases in Bangalore and San Francisco.

At the moment his firm has 96,000 data collectors across countries in Latin America and Southeast Asia.

Humyn Labs filters the thousands of hours of video supplied by those collectors, selecting the most useful content. It's then labelled so the AI knows what it is looking at and packaged in a format that can be used by clients.

"You need to source across a variety of factories, a variety of tasks, in different geographies because if you want to build deployment for US factories or US houses, you'd want environments that are a proxy for that. Residential houses in India may not be the right context for building a robotics use case for the US."

The factory workers teaching robots how to work like humans

But are workers collecting data for machines that will one day do their jobs?

At the moment it's too early to tell says Bino Paul, senior professor at the School of Management and Labour Studies at the Tata Institute of Social Sciences (TISS) in Mumbai.

For some workers AI might be a useful tool, but robots could automate other jobs, he says.

The problem is that, if machines do start to take over, there will be little workers can do about.

"By the time workers realise what they have participated in, things may have gone too far," he says.

Like many others, he wants governments to regulate AI.

"AI cannot be viewed merely as a technical system. We need to develop a socio-technical system in which ethics is upheld through human agency, ensuring that AI delivers beneficence rather than maleficence."

Despite concerns over where AI is heading, money is still being poured into its development.

Some of that trickles down to workers in India, where there is no shortage of workers prepared to wear a camera for a few dollars a day.

Mohammed Arman has been working in the textiles industry in Bangalore for 10 years.

"Garments stitching and cutting. I know this work so well I could probably do it with my eyes closed. Cloth, thread, needle, machine after 10 years, your hands just know what to do before your mind even tells them," he says.

He has recently started wearing a head-mounted camera that records his work.

"For me, it's simple. This is survival. I have a family back home in Bihar, and every rupee I send back matters. If someone offers me extra money to wear a camera while I do the exact same work I've been doing for 10 years, I'm not going to say no.

"It doesn't cost me anything. I'm already doing the stitching, already doing the cutting. Now I just get paid a little more for it," he says.

Mohammed Arman has been filming his tailoring work

While there is demand for such footage it will be collected somewhere.

"India's current role in this industry is largely as a supplier of raw material - people strap on cameras, record themselves doing ordinary tasks, and that footage gets shipped off to labs abroad," Agarwal says.

But he argues the value of that kind of work will quickly become eroded, as more companies compete for collection contracts.

"India can't afford to stay a footage supplier. It needs to move up the value chain: from recording raw video to owning the pipeline that turns that video into something usable - cleaning it, structuring it, validating it, and ultimately setting the standards for what counts as high-quality training data," he says.

Jolly Arifur has been filming domestic work for a few hours a day

For some people collecting data is attractive part time work.

Science teacher Jolly Arifur was looking for work she could do from home that would fit around her young son.

"For the past seven months, I strapped a rig-mounted camera on my head while doing my routine domestic work scrubbing, cooking, sweeping tile floors, and folding laundry."

Arifur, who lives in Guntakal, Anantapur, fits the recording work around her teaching.

"I get paid two to three dollars per hour. If I record three to five hours a day, that's a good amount in hand it's become an extra source of income for me, on top of teaching. I'm managing both honestly, fantastically."

The virtual worlds where robots are trained

#184. A massive trade just happened in a leading mortgage lender

For more options-trading reaction to the rally, click the link at the top of the page for Options Action LIVE featuring CNBC contributors Michael Khouw and Brent Kochuba alongside host Oliver Renick.

Stocks are surging after midterms brought a Democratic blue wave and options traders are responding by selling volatility and embracing a rotation away from big-tech trades.The S&P 500 added just shy of 2% by midday Wednesday, recovering losses from Friday and Monday. The move in its corresponding options prices was even bigger, with the Cboe Volatility index falling about four points to below 17, the biggest one-day drop in the volatility gauge since at least July.Crude oil prices fell as WTI traded below $90 and energy stocks dropped 5.6% for the biggest decline in the S&P 500. Options on the State Street Energy Select Sector SPDR ETF (XLE) jumped to more than three times the 30-day average, with 57% puts by volume and the big-money trades concentrated in bearish put-buyers. The top trade in XLE was the 62.5-strike put expiring Jan. 15, suggesting it could be a slow rollover for what has been the best-performing sector of 2026, SpotGamma data show.With the drop in crude oil, a handful of inflation and interest-rate related trades got some bullish attention. Consumer discretionary stocks rallied almost 5% but options volume in the SPDR sector fund XLY was below average and volume was split between puts and calls, according to Cboe LiveVol data.Instead, there was some big action in a few housing trades such as Rocket Companies, the mortgage lender and Redfin owner that fell almost 50% this year before turning around last month. Options volume on Rocket was double the average with a strong bullish bias. Of the 110,000 options traded, 90% were calls and more were likely initiated by buyers than sellers. Bulls traded the bulls like the 14 and 16-strike calls expiring Nov. 20 the most.Opposite the drop in crude oil was a rally in solar energy companies, extending recent gains and extending unusual options activity that preceded the midterms.The Invesco Solar ETF (TAN) added another 9%, trading the highest since July. Options volume was more than five times the average and 80% of the volume was in calls. The most popular trade in TAN was the 48-strike call betting on a 6% rally through Nov. 20th.On the tech side, options flows looked bearish in the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH), which fell 2.6% to the lowest since May. Options volume was twice the average, 70% of it puts, with the 475-strike put expiring Nov. 13 the most popular contract bought by volume.

#183. 'An annoying buzz': The search for stealthier drones

Amazon Prime Air is expanding to 500 US towns and cities

Nature lover Ashley Schmidt no longer spends much time in her garden, in the Dallas suburb of North Garland.

She's also given up on cycling to the local parks where she previously enjoyed taking close-up photos of the bugs, lizards and toads.

It's all because she can't stand the sound of the delivery drones that fly overhead, depositing her neighbours' online orders.

"They emit this very annoying buzz. It triggers my fight or flight response," says the 44-year-old administrative assistant.

"You can hear the drones on approach and you can hear them inside your home when they pass directly overhead and then you hear them as they leave," she says.

Amazon says it is working to reduce the noise of its drone fleet

Schmidt is one of a growing number of people living with the noise of drones dispatched by companies including Amazon and Walmart as they seek to speed up home deliveries.

Delivery drone services, not long ago a novelty, are becoming part of everyday life for many people in the US.

Among them, Amazon Prime Air is in the process of expanding deliveries to nearly 500 cities and towns, delivering items that fit into a space the size of a shoe box. It has also launched to customers in and around Darlington in the north of England.

Smaller drone delivery services are popping up too: in some US cities, customers can order flying treats from their local pizza joint or ice cream parlour.

Even in cities where noise is already a steady part of life, the sound of drones seems to irk in a particular way.

While the steady passing of traffic might blend into the background, the sudden whine of a drone's rotors can pierce the everyday hum.

The sound tends to be "particularly noticeable, even when overall sound levels are relatively low," says Antonio Torija Martinez, professor in acoustic engineering and psychoacoustics at the University of Salford.

Unlike planes, drones operate at low altitudes and may fly directly over residential areas.

"They can expose communities that have not previously experienced significant aircraft noise, and who may therefore be more sensitive to changes in their local soundscape," says Torija Martinez.

He points to research showing that people respond strongly to a noise when they consider the source to be "novel, intrusive or frequent". People who benefit from a drone delivery service, for example, may be less bothered by the noise.

However, complaints about the sound of drones are likely to grow, he warns: "At the moment we are seeing some of these problems localised in some areas, butâ¦ as the drone sector expands, more and more of these complaints will happen."

In the US, the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) regulates commercial drones, but it has come under fire for allowing noisy drone delivery operations. The FAA says operators and local community leaders have primary responsibility for addressing any community concerns.

Greenjets encloses some of the blades on its drone inside a pod

Might better technology muffle the irritating buzz?

One startup, Greenjets, set out to tackle both "noise level and the frequency of that noise," as Darren James, future technologies lead at the company, puts it.

The London-based business has designed a propulsion system it claims can reduce drone noise by at least two thirds.

While standard drones use spinning propellers that slice through open air, creating that high-pitched buzz, Greenjets encloses spinning blades inside a pod, to muffle the sound and direct the noise away from the ground.

Its system also quietens things down through slower rotor speed and by using materials that reduce vibration noise.

Making quieter drones isn't just about keeping the noise down for neighbours who are already sick of airborne deliveries.

Greenjets' low-noise tech has been used by police and harbour security teams in Germany. It has also helped conservationists in Africa, who are seeking to track poachers from above without being detected.

Battlefield drone operators also have good reason to keep the noise down, to avoid alerting the enemy of the approach of their unmanned vehicles.

"There are some efforts to reduce the sound of drones. The US Special Forces are interested, at an experimental stage," says Robert Tollast, a research fellow at Whitehall think tank The Royal United Services Institute (RUSI).

But he stresses that drone speed, rather than stealthiness, tends to come first for larger drones used in the war between Russia and Ukraine. "Make it faster, not less quiet, seems to be the approach," says Tollast.

Sharrow Engineering developed twisted propellers that reduce noise

For Detroit-based entrepreneur, Greg Sharrow, it was the challenge of broadcasting a classical music performance back in 2012 that set him on the path to quieter drone design.

He needed to film a violinist from a tricky angle and a drone seemed the only solution â except that it would be far too noisy.

As a starting point, "I knew that most of the noise from the drone was coming from the propeller, not the motor," he says. Specifically, it was the tips of the blades that he realised were creating the noise as they swiped through the air.

"I asked myself a very simple question, what would a tipless propeller look like and how would it function?"

The result was to create a propeller with twisted loop-shaped blades with no points.

It turned out it was easier to test his propeller in water than it was to repeatedly fly a drone, which led Sharrow to initially develop a less noisy boat propeller. Now, however, his company, Sharrow Engineering, is focusing on developing "ultra-quiet" drone rotors.

"We have validated that the technology works in drone applications and that it is dramatically quieter," says Sharrow.

Companies at the forefront of drone deliveries say they are also working to reduce the noise of their fleets. A spokesperson for Amazon says it has made "measurable sound improvements" to its drones and its latest designs use custom-designed propellers "that produce almost half the perceived sound of the prior generation, with a milder tone".

Drone maker Wing, which provides its vehicles for Walmart deliveries, says its propellers are designed to minimise noise. It also varies its flight paths to prevent repeated overflights over any single home or neighbourhood.

For Schmidt, fed up with the noise of drones, the only truly satisfactory solution would be to halt drone deliveries in her area. "I would like them to completely stop until they figure out how their services stop being a nuisance," she says.

X-planes: Are they needed in the new era of drones?

#181. 'I live with a ghost' - inside the haunted homes attracting bargain hunters

The previous occupant of Ryua's apartment, pictured here, sadly took their own life

From the moment Ryua first walked into her rental apartment in Tokyo she believed it to be haunted.

"When I visited the place for a viewing there was a man standing there, glaring at me," she says.

"Since the estate agent couldn't see him, I realised he must be a ghost."

While someone more squeamish would probably have quickly left the flat never to return, Ryua chose to go ahead and move in.

"I'm a bit of a contrarian, so I decided I wanted to become friends with this glaring male ghost," adds Ryua, who works as an aidoru, a young entertainer, and who doesn't wish to share her surname.

Four years later, and still in the same apartment, she says she continues to experience paranormal activity on a daily basis. She says she hears endless footsteps and the ghost talking.

Ryua says that she shares her Tokyo apartment with a ghost

Although Japan has increasingly marked Halloween, external in recent years, many Japanese people take ghosts seriously all year round. The country has a word, "yuurei", which means a lingering, troubled spirit.

These are the ghosts of people who died in violent or tragic circumstances - those who were murdered, killed themselves, or simply were lonely and died alone. If they died at home, then the belief is that their yuurei won't go away.

For house hunters who are not superstitious or squeamish it means there are big bargains to be found. There is a whole category of dwellings called jiko bukken, which means "stigmatised" or "incident" properties.

Under guidelines issued by the government in 2021, Japanese estate agents must disclose suicide, murder, death by fire, and any death where the body was not discovered for an extended period of time.

For rental properties this information must be disclosed for three years, and it must be revealed indefinitely for any property offered for sale.

By contrast, in the UK there is no legal requirement, external to reveal that a person died in a property. And in the US, it differs from state to state., external For example, in California you have to reveal if a person has died in the property in the past three years, while in Arizona and Oregon you do not.

Ryua was told that a man had killed himself in her apartment. It meant that she gets an almost 30% discount on her rent on her tiny, 25 sq m bedsit in the fashionable ward of Setagaya. She says she pays 45,000 yen ($285; Â£213) a month, instead of what should have been 62,000 yen.

While a house where there had been a murder or suicide would probably be harder to sell or rent out anywhere in the world, Tokyo estate agent Erik Oskamp says that Japanese people are particularly superstitious and averse to stigmatised properties.

"[Even] an apartment next to a cemetery will be difficult to rent out, especially if you can see the tombs from the kitchen," explains Oskamp. "The worst thing is if a suicide has happened in the apartment. Even if somebody has jumped from the roof, many renters will move out. Other issues are a serious crime or a lonely death."

The latter issue, that of a person dying alone, would not generally affect the price of a property in, say, the UK or the US.

But in rural Kagoshima, in Japan's southwest, it enabled Diane Tincher to buy a large three-bedroom house for about half of what would have otherwise been the asking price.

The 69-year-old American has lived in Japan for nearly 40 years, working as a tour guide. She says that the estate agent had been straight with her â there was a heavy discount as an elderly lady had died alone, thereby stigmatising the property.

Tincher paid 20m yen for the house, in a location where similar properties usually sell for up to 45m yen.

"I don't believe in ghosts. But I know that the Japanese are very superstitious," she says. "If there is a ghost I'm thankful to her every day... to live in such a splendid and affordable home."

Diane Tincher says she got a 50% reduction on her home in rural Japan

Researching the house, Tincher had found it on a website called Oshimaland, which lists jiko bukken properties, giving details of the events that occurred. It was launched in 2005 covering Tokyo, before expanding across Japan and then internationally in 2011.

It is crowdsourced, meaning that members of the public add the homes and their details.

Diane Tincher got a huge discount on her home in rural Japan

Teru Oshima, the founder of Oshimaland, says that in the past such properties were "not very attractive options" in Japan.

Yet as house prices have surged in the country in recent years, as well as a big increase in the wider cost of living, one 2025 survey found that more than half of Japanese people would now rent a jiko bukken if it was cheap., external

Back in Setagaya, Ryua says she is happy living with her ghostly flatmate. "I believe that ghosts were once living human beings, so they deserve our respect," she says.

#179. Americans have said 'NO TO WAR,' Iran speaker says as Democrats take Congress

Iran's speaker of parliament Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf said Americans have said "NO TO WAR," as projections from U.S. midterm elections showed that Democrats have flipped both houses of Congress.

Posting on X after projections showed that Democrats will have a majority in the House and Senate over the coming two years, Ghalibaf said that "Conscience or the cost of living, Americans said NO TO WAR, and we endorse this message."

"If the [U.S. government] betrays their public will to serve international paymasters and blackmailers, it will learn our NO comes with a Nightmare."

The X account of Iran's Supreme Leader Ayatollah Mojtaba Khamenei posted: "The process of the US's collapse has accelerated."

"If there are still those who entertain the slightest hope that the hegemonic system may change behavior or shifts in its political parties may bring about change, or that by making concessions to the US, they can persuade it to abandon its hostility, they're gravely mistaken," the account added.

Last Saturday, Khamenei had called for Tehran's armed forces to meet "any level or type of threat" with "an immediate, impactful, and geographically unrestricted response."

The Iran war has been a central issue for voters in the midterms, after U.S. President Donald Trump launched joint strikes with Israel against the Islamic Republic in February. The conflict has pushed up energy prices across the U.S., which has led to cost of living concerns.

Trump is also blaming the Iran war in part for the Democratic sweep of both chambers of Congress, telling the Wall Street Journal that "Iran was a piece of it" when asked if the war contributed to the Republicans losses.

However, he maintains that prosecuting the war was the right decision, saying, "I don't think about it. I did the right thing. I would have done it again."

Washington has not explicitly stated it plans to resume attacks on Iran, but reports from The Wall Street Journal and The New York Times indicate the U.S. is preparing for possible renewed military actions after the midterm elections.

With their new majorities, Democrats might be able to push through a War Powers Resolution, which will direct Trump to end the conflict with Iran should Congress not authorize it.

In June, both chambers — which were then Republican controlled — passed a concurrent resolution to end the conflict, although a concurrent resolution is not binding and does not have the force of law. Previous attempts to impose a binding War Powers Resolution had been defeated.
