#140. Graduates are managing AI - but are they learning much else?

Managing AI agents is a big part of Lotte Underwood's job

Recent university graduate Lotte Underwood is a public relations executive with a twist - she spends more time using AI than actually doing PR.

Underwood, 25, joined London-based PR agency Fight or Flight in November last year, in the new role of "AI associate" after completing a degree and then a masters in marketing.

She's part of a recently established, dedicated AI team that has built around 30 AI agents. Specific tasks include industry research, client reporting, media interview preparation, helping clients create clearer briefs, and image generation.

A big part of Underwood's job is to manage those agents, check their outputs and feed those to the team members who are dealing directly with clients and the media.

Another key responsibility of hers is to monitor how clients appear in the responses from AI tools like ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and others.

"A huge part is being in client meetings and calls to work out what is actually going to be most helpful from these agents," says Underwood.

AI is seen as one of the main factors behind graduate job cuts, but companies are also creating dedicated AI-specific graduate roles like Underwood's.

Dillan Patel, for example, joined engineering software company Stride 18 months ago as an intern. A business management graduate, he was put to work developing an internal chatbot designed to capture the knowledge of a senior staff member transitioning into retirement.

Patel, 24, is now an AI automation analyst, tasked with developing further internal agents to provide specific information to individual teams, while also identifying which manual or repetitive tasks across the company could be done by AI.

"My role focuses on finding practical ways to use technology, automation, data and AI to improve how we operate," says Coventry-based Patel.

Debra Maxwell is looking for "AI disruptors"

Such AI savvy graduates are also in demand from companies like ArvatoConnect, an outsourced contact centre company.

CEO Debra Maxwell says the company's usual graduate intake is on hold this year, but she plans to resume it next year with a bigger focus on AI.

"We would be looking for more AI-disruptor type roles, where you can challenge us as an organisation to think more holistically about where AI can be applied and where we could grow our business from," says London-based Maxwell.

"We see our graduates as not filling administrative roles, but actually adding value to the organisation in a different way."

Maxwell adds: "For example, one of our recent graduates is using AI to do deep dives on specific markets, and provide more information for when we're bidding [for new contracts]. It's basically giving him a more rounded role."

If AI can make a graduate job bigger and more integral to the organisation, Maxwell argues that companies can create a more loyal, engaged workforce that is able to contribute more meaningfully, much earlier.

Research from consultancy PwC found that some graduate roles are assuming more responsibilities because of AI, as it allows companies to demand more senior abilities from junior hires.

For the consultancy's 2026 AI Jobs Barometer, external, it analysed 2.4 million entry-level jobs in the US. It found that the entry-level roles most influenced by AI are now seven times more likely to require traditionally senior-level skills. These include motivational leadership, strategic decision making and team building.

Those entry-level roles with higher expectations have in fact grown 35% since 2019, PwC found, while other entry-level roles actually shrank by 10%.

Cian Clinton uses around ten AI agents for work tasks

Cian Clinton, who joined consultancy Valliance last September after graduating from a Bachelor of Science in 2024, says the incorporation of AI into his role makes him feel less like a graduate, and more like an equal team member. He uses around 10 AI agents daily for different tasks.

"Your ideas and your creativity are a lot more valued because you actually get to implement those firsthand, and the responsibility you take on is a lot greater because of that," says London-based Clinton, 23.

"I am now taking on responsibilities which would usually take me four or five years to get to."

His career progression, he believes, will now be faster as a result.

"AI is killing the 'pyramid' approachâ¦progression is no longer based on your knowledge bank. Now it's more based on your critical thinking and understanding of the processes," says Clinton.

There's no doubt Underwood, Patel and Clinton are developing valuable AI knowledge which can help them across their careers.

But with their time at work so focused on AI, does it matter how much - or how little - time they're spending learning about the professional field they're supposed to be operating in?

Underwood says she'd like to still pick up the traditional PR skills her role isn't currently focused on, but is confident of her future in the agency's growing AI team.

"There's certain things that I haven't had the chance to do yet, like directly pitching to the media. I absolutely plan to do this," she says.

Graduates becoming too AI-centric is an issue for Simon Pratt, chief operating officer at Portman Finance Group. He says being able to use AI effectively is important, but the company's two-year graduate programme is fully centred on "learning the trade".

"Our graduates rotate across four areas at six-month intervals, working directly alongside senior relationship managers to learn their trade," Northampton-based Pratt explains.

"They'll learn all of our financial products, how to draft credit papers for submission to lenders, and how to build meaningful relationships with our customers. They'll progress to running transactions from start to finish."

Claire Reid, PwC UK's chief technology and innovation officer, believes graduate roles that are too AI heavy won't necessarily provide graduates long-term career progression.

"There will be no graduate job now that doesn't require a baseline of AI literacy," says Glasgow-based Reid.

"But AI skills on their own are not going to help you in your career. You've got to couple that with that sector knowledge of what that client actually wants."

Back in Coventry, Dillan Patel is confident his AI work will continue to be valuable, and he wants to keep building on it.

"I don't think I'd want to explore different positions within the business. Through my involvement in AI-related projects, I already work closely with different areas of the business and I'm gaining valuable knowledge and experience along the way," he says.

#138. Snap shares fall over 10% on third-quarter revenue miss

Snap shares sank over 10% when the Snapchat parent missed on revenue and daily active users in its third-quarter earnings report Wednesday.

Here's how the company did compared with analysts' expectations:

Sales in Snap's third quarter jumped 14% year-over-year while net income came in at $56 million, compared to the $104 million net loss it logged the prior year.

Snap's third-quarter adjusted earnings came in at $383 million, topping the $337 million that analysts were projecting, according to StreetAccount.

The Snapchat parent said fourth-quarter revenue should come in between $1.95 billion and $1.99 billion. The midpoint of that figure is higher than Wall Street's estimates of $1.95 billion.

Fourth-quarter adjusted earnings are expected to come in between $490 million and $525 million. Analysts were expecting that figure to be $498 million.

Snap executives said in an investor letter that the company plans for globally monthly active users to become its "primary audience metric" starting in its first quarter for 2027. The company also said it has 977 MAU for the quarter.

"Daily engagement has historically been stable as a percentage of monthly engagement, but we do not intend to optimize Snapchat around daily use at the expense of long-term value for our community," the investor letter said. "A longer-term horizon better aligns our reported metrics with the product and safety decisions that strengthen relationships, create lasting utility, and support broader, more durable audience growth."

Snap chief commercial officer Ronan Harris told analysts during an earnings call that lower spending by unspecified consumer packaged goods, or CPG, companies impacted third-quarter sales. Harris attributed the curbed CPG spending to "macro pressures in the U.S. and also the Middle East.""As we look ahead, we're seeing very positive signs of growth from diversified verticals like financial services in the U.S.," Harris said. "So part of our strategy here is to look outside of the pressures in CPG and find traction in these other verticals."

#137. Binance says fake job interviews used as part of $6.3bn crypto hack

Hackers who stole a record $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) from Binance used fake job interviews and a compromised internal AI tool, the firm has revealed.

Initial findings published by the company, which operates the world's largest cryptocurrency exchange, said hackers gained access to its systems through a "sophisticated, multi-stage campaign".

It said they used a range of techniques such as malicious software downloads to compromise the computers of Binance engineers and gain control over tools to drain its wallet of billions of dollars worth of crypto.

Binance called it a "serious and highly sophisticated security incident" and apologised, saying "this should not have happened".

In a post on X, external, Binance said hackers targeted its engineers using social engineering techniques.

These see hackers use approaches such as phishing to trick people into parting with sensitive or personal details, which can then be used to try and access their accounts.

Binance said as part of the hack, one of its engineers was contacted through a legitimate job-hunting platform and invited to bogus job interviews with fake company representatives.

It said the perpetrators used "more advanced and deceptive" methods to build trust with him, such as sharing social media accounts which contained personal images.

The engineer in question was instructed to download malware masquerading as an "assignment" during a second interview, which deployed malicious code with "obfuscation techniques" evading detection.

Binance said hackers then leveraged the engineer's compromised computer and an internal AI tool to gain control over two other engineers' laptops, and execute the next steps of the attack.

The incident led the firm to suspend withdrawals for customers globally, meaning users could not get their cryptocurrency out of the platform until the service was restored on Sunday.

Customers withdrew billions of dollars worth of assets after it reopened.

"We apologise for the concern, disruption and uncertainty it caused our users, partners and the wider industry," it said.

The company added it had taken a range of steps following the incident, including working with law enforcement to trace and freeze affected assets.

It said it had also set aside more than 100% of the funds needed to cover the loss, and said 98% of the token assets impacted had been "replenished" as of Saturday.

It is thought to be the largest crypto hack to date.

What we know about the $6.3bn Binance hack

US crime agencies, including the FBI and Secret Service, have been assisting Binance in tracing the crypto taken from the platform's own wallets.

The agencies, along with a range of cyber analysts, said the hack - like similar breaches before it - bore the signs of having been orchestrated by North Korea.

Pyongyang hit back at the claims on Tuesday, denying it was behind the hack.

Kim Yo Jong, a top North Korean official and the sister of leader Kim Jong Un, said the accusations were another "sinister attempt" by the US to damage the counry's public image.

The country has consistently denied being behind a number of major cyber-attacks it has been blamed for in recent years.

The TraderTraitor unit, which is also known as the Lazarus Group, was blamed for a then-record $1.5bn hack of crypto exchange Bybit in 2025.

Binance had previously not responded to the BBC's queries about North Korea's alleged involvement in the hack.

But on Tuesday, the company wrote in its post on X, external: "The latest information released by the Federal Bureau of Investigation indicates that the Democratic People's Republic of Korea is responsible for this incident."

As part of its security efforts following the attack, Binance said it would also increase investment in AI tools which can detect security threats, as well as engage third-party cyber experts to carry out a full-scale review of the incident.

It also said that it would seek to establish a security research fund and work with leading AI and security firms on targeted initiatives.

#136. Kellogg's admits snap, crackle and pop is quieter after uproar over Rice Krispies recipe change

Kellogg's has told the BBC the "snap, crackle and pop" sound made by its Rice Krispies may be noticeably quieter after an uproar over the cereal giant's recipe change.

Rice Krispies and Coco Pops are no longer being made with whole rice grains, with the tiny toasted puffs being made using corn flour and rice flour instead.

A Kellogg's spokesperson said retaining the iconic sound had been a "huge focus" when designing the new recipe, but that customers "might notice that the sound may be quieter and not as instant as before".

The firm was responding to complaints online that the taste of the breakfast staples had changed, with some fans adding they missed the "pop" sound.

Kellogg's said it had adapted the recipes due to "changing weather conditions" that have disrupted supplies of some grains and cereals.

The cereal giant said the new versions had been "carefully assessed for taste, appearance and texture", but some shoppers said their children would no longer eat them.

Some expressed dismay that the taste and texture were "totally different" and dubbed the change "grim" and "disgusting".

"There's no snap, crackle and pop in these," a parent named Dani wrote on TikTok. "My toddler won't eat them."

Lauren, also posting on TikTok, added that she had been eating Rice Krispies every day for breakfast "for years and years".

"I had a bowl of the new recipe and you can immediately tell the difference," she said. "They don't have the snapping sound when you put in the milk."

Jessicake meanwhile said they tasted "weird and stale".

The "Snap, Crackle and Pop" - made when milk is added to the puffed rice - has been a centrepiece of the brand's marketing since the 1950s.

Kellogg's insisted the snap, crackle and pop is "still there, but consumers might notice that the sound may be quieter and not as instant as before".

Recipe changes are notoriously difficult for big brands. Chocolate makers are constantly accused of reducing cocoa content in their products and debates rage online over whether ketchup ingredients have changed.

When Scottish fizzy drink Irn Bru cut its sugar content in 2018, fans famously stockpiled crates of the old version.

No changes are being made to Rice Krispies products in the US and Canada, which are owned by a separate company.

Kellogg's said the recipe change in the UK would help "maintain consistent quality" as extreme weather takes a toll on crops and pushes up ingredient prices.

Extreme weather in Spain in 2022 and 2023 in particular had reduced availability of the types of rice the company uses for its breakfast cereals, the company said

Rice Krispies, launched nearly a century ago, are sold around the world and come in variations such as vanilla and strawberry flavours in some markets alongside the widespread chocolate-flavoured Coco Pops version.

"We appreciate that even small changes to a much-loved product can feel noticeable and that's why we have approached this with a lot of care and consideration," Kellogg's said.

The firm said it appreciated the customer feedback and would "continue to look at ways to make [its] products even better in the future".

The cereals have already undergone some change, with reductions in salt content in the 1990s and lowered sugar in 2018. The company said Rice Krispies and Coco Pops will continue to meet UK food standards on levels of fat, salt and sugar in food.

A backlash against processed foods in recent years, especially those low in fibre and high in sugar, has put cereals generally under the spotlight from healthy eating advocates.

Kellogg's stressed the cereals contain no artificial colours, flavours or sweeteners and the nutritional benefits in each bowl, such as fibre, Vitamin D and iron are staying the same.

However, the new recipes mean packaging will now include a "contains wheat gluten" warning. It also continues to contain barley-malt extract.

Watch: How does El NinÌo affect world weather?

Kellogg's said it has not changed the recipe for any of its other cereals.

Many parts of the world have been affected by droughts, floods and heatwaves this year due to the El NiÃ±o weather phenomenon. The weather event is expected to severely affect crop production from South East Asia in particular. The region produces more than a quarter of the world's rice.

The World Meteorological Agency expects the record-breaking El NiÃ±o will last until at least 2027.

That will intensify pressure on food manufacturing companies, already looking at ways to source reliable and affordable ingredients.

The original Kellogg company separated into two distinct firms in 2023.

The largest is Kellanova, which focuses on snacks, cereals, noodles and other food products like Eggo, Pringles and Pop-Tarts sold globally. It is now owned by Mars, which makes confectionery and pet food.

The other business, WK Kellogg's, operates in North America and has since been taken over by Italian chocolate giant Ferrero.

#134. Figma sinks after earnings beat as company prioritizes long-term growth with AI changes

Figma shares tumbled 7% in extended trading after the company reported third-quarter results and guidance that surpassed Wall Street's predictions.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

Figma's revenue grew about 43% year over year in the quarter, according to a statement. Net loss narrowed to $81.3 million, or 15 cents per share, compared with a loss of $1.10 billion, or $2.72 per share, a year earlier.

The company's 12% adjusted operating margin exceeded the 6.1% consensus among analysts polled by StreetAccount.

Executives lifted the full-year forecast. Figma now anticipates an 11% operating margin, up from 9% as of August, and $1.497 billion to $1.499 billion in revenue, compared with $1.463 billion to $1.467 billion before. Analysts surveyed by LSEG were looking for $1.471 billion in 2026 revenue.

The company sees fourth-quarter revenue growth slowing to about 33%. Analysts polled by LSEG had predicted 28% growth.

Figma's announcement comes a day after highly valued design software startup Canva said it would cut 22% of its employees.

Shares have come down about 41% so far in 2026, while the S&P 500 index has gained 12%. Earlier this year, investors became concerned that artificial intelligence models could pose a competitive risk to Figma. Across much of software, AI panic has largely subsided as incumbents have posted persistent growth.

Figma wants to benefit from the rising adoption of AI to build websites and apps. During the quarter, the company started giving more AI credits to clients while maintaining prices.

"What that means is you're potentially trading off near-term revenue, investing in your customers, and then allowing yourself to then grow durably over the long term," Figma's finance chief, Praveer Melwani, told CNBC in an interview on Wednesday.

The number of weekly users working at organizations that spend over $10,000 on Figma who used AI credits increased by almost 50% during the quarter, Figma said in the statement. The share of work the company's own AI model performs doubled, and the rate of people undoing AI-generated work has fallen.

"If there's certain actions that we can power for our customers at lower latency, lower cost, or similar quality to third party, we're open to that as well," Melwani said.

Figma is increasing its focus on cybersecurity, said Dylan Field, Figma's co-founder and CEO. In recent months, AI agents have infiltrated systems at Hugging Face and other companies.

Clients aren't asking Figma to become a security vendor, but the company must scrutinize "the security of all of our vendors right now," Field said.

#133. Trump hammers Fox News, blaming network for GOP losses as ratings slide

President Donald Trump turned against Fox News on Tuesday night in a string of posts on Truth Social while Democratic wins rolled in during a historic midterm election.

Trump blamed the network, which he's long championed, for Republican losses, saying in one post on his social media network that it "bears responsibility for these losses by constantly and consistently putting on the Dumocrat opposition."

Democrats took control of both the Senate and House of Representatives, flipping seats in states usually favorable to Republicans, including Texas, South Carolina and Kansas.

Trump also pointed to Fox's recent ratings as evidence of the network's decline.

"FoxNews Ratings are way down (27%!) because they're spending too much time covering the Trump Deranged Dumocrats! All of those people who used to watch Fox, and no longer do, get it. It's them, not me," he posted.

Fox News did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

In a press release last week, Fox News reported 1.1 million average viewers for October, down about 27% from the same month a year prior.

Fox Corp. CEO Lachlan Murdoch noted the decline during the company's quarterly earnings call on Monday, citing "viewer fatigue."

However, Murdoch said such a shift is expected in the lead-up to a midterm election with a second-term president and said it was not the result of viewers turning to competitors' coverage.

"Those viewers are not going to any other news channel. They're not switching to MS NOW or to CNN," he said. "They're just fatigued and watching slightly less television overall."

Versant-owned MS NOW and Skydance-owned CNN both reported primetime viewership was up by double digits in October compared with the year-ago period. However, both networks still had smaller total-day average audiences than Fox News.

MS NOW had 588,000 average viewers in October across all day parts, and CNN had 402,000, according to Nielsen data cited by Fox.

Despite Fox News' year-over-year drop in viewership, the company reported an otherwise strong quarter in what Murdoch called an "outstanding start to fiscal 2027."

Fox Corp., which includes the linear TV networks as well as entertainment and sports assets and a streaming business, reported revenue for its fiscal first quarter of $4.57 billion, up 22% year over year.

The company pointed to strength in advertising revenue, which increased 47%, buoyed by strong political advertising and the FIFA Men's World Cup, which aired in part on Fox platforms across June and July.

Trump is a frequent critic of media companies, especially broadcasters that he views as being biased against him. While he's repeatedly taken aim at NBC and Disney-owned ABC, the president has had a much friendlier relationship with Fox and its networks.

Trump appeared on Fox News' "Hannity" on Monday night ahead of the election.

Disclosure: CNBC and MS NOW are divisions of Versant Media.

#132. UK and EU agree draft deal to scrap Brexit red tape over food exports

The UK and EU have agreed a draft deal on removing post-Brexit checks from food and agriculture exports, ahead of the UK-EU summit later this month.

The BBC understands the 200-page draft text for this so called "SPS" [sanitary and phytosanitary] agreement has been circulated to EU nations in Brussels for consideration at a meeting on Wednesday.

Similar texts for deals on emissions trading and a youth travel and work scheme have also been circulated alongside a 15-page draft joint statement for UK and EU top leaders.

The food deal is the most significant part of the so-called Brexit reset agreed so far, removing checks and red tape that hit exporters after the UK exited the EU's single market.

As a result of the agreement, the government claims the UK economy will eventually see Â£5.1bn worth of annual benefits.

Speaking during the Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting (Chogm) in Antigua and Barbuda, the prime minister said a UK-EU deal could add up to 0.4% extra on GDP.

The deal could have the following impact:

Exports to the EU of fresh sausages, burgers, shellfish from domestic waters and seed potatoes would be able to resume

It should remove the need for Â£200 Export Health Certificates, which confirm the product meets the health requirements of the country it is heading to

It should also end the need for Â£25 Phytosanitary certificates, which show that plants or plant products are free from pests and diseases

Inspector fees, organic certificates, some port fees and other checks for exports and imports to and from the EU could be removed

It will bring back passports for British pets being taken into the EU

In return, the UK is expected to commit to observing and adapting to existing EU regulations on plant, animal and food trade standards - a process called "dynamic alignment".

Each of the elements on the table was up for discussion under Sir Keir Starmer, and the summit would have taken place by now had Labour not replaced its leader in July.

Under Andy Burnham, Britain's approach to post-Brexit relations with the EU have shifted significantly. Sir Keir said the UK would not rejoin the bloc in his lifetime, while Burnham has said he would like to see Britain back in the EU in his.

At Chogm on Tuesday, Burnham said: "I think people in Britain would expect me to take that with both hands, given where we've been in recent times, and that's what I'm doing."

Pressed on whether it would be acceptable for the UK to have its rules on food trade made by the EU, he said: "We're not in the EU, therefore we do have to work with them in a different way.

"But if that can get growth, if that can help our farmers, if that can remove red tape from British businesses, I think most people would expect me to go to that summit and negotiate the best deal that I can.

"And that's what I've been doing in recent weeks, leaning in, being positive, having a really constructive relationship with our European partners and hopefully we can land a good deal for Britain in terms of growth but also I hope more collaboration on controlling immigration."

Jon Walsh of Bio&Me said his firm had stopped exporting yoghurt due to vets' fees

Jon Walsh, the chief executive and co-founder of Bio&Me, which makes granolas, mueslis and yoghurts, told BBC 5 Live the draft deal sounded like "really good news".

"We're just a small company but when we were trying to export our yoghurts before, every time we'd send a pallet we had to pay a vet to inspect it and that was Â£140, and that meant that you'd lose money... so we had to stop."

The company also had problems exporting granola and overnight oats, with some shipments being returned.

"It was so frustrating because it was the same person filling out the same form and yet half would make it through and half wouldn't," Walsh said.

"You just kind of give up because you're losing money."

In an interview with ITV, Burnham said "public opinion has changed nationally" since the 2016 Brexit vote, but added he would "not run ahead of public opinion" on the issue.

"The public need to be the boss when it comes to whatever happens with Brexit," he added.

Burnham also said he had not proposed the return of freedom of movement between the UK and EU.

Asked by the BBC about the UK's relationship with the EU, Tory Leader Kemi Badenoch said that while we should "work closely", "dynamic alignment" with the EU would be a red line and "actually take us backwards".

Liberal Democrat Europe spokesperson Al Pinkerton said the draft deal was a "patch, not a fix".

"The real solution is to rejoin the single market and to establish a customs union with the EU," he added.

Reform UK's business spokesman Richard Tice said: "One of the key benefits of Brexit was that Britain no longer had to adhere to ridiculous EU rules.

"Returning to any form of alignment with Europe would crush Britain's competitiveness and force us to surrender sovereignty over trade policy, putting trade deals worth hundreds of billions of pounds at risk."

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen with Prime Minister Andy Burnham

While UK rules remain mostly aligned with EU ones, there are some areas where England has diverged from the EU rule book, for example on "precision breeding" of plants, previously known as gene editing. In recent weeks, agreement has been struck on transition arrangements while the EU's new regulations come in to force.

The government has already announced a European Partnerships Bill which will provide a legal mechanism for the application of new EU rules and regulations. This will also allow for the necessary alignments for the linking of the UK emissions trading scheme with the EU's, and save the need for UK exporters to pay the EU's new carbon border tax.

The draft agreements will be discussed at a meeting of EU ambassadors called Coreper on Wednesday, with some key details on the planned numbers in a Youth Experience Scheme to be finalised at a political level in the coming days. A discount on full foreign student fees for EU students is likely to be pushed to next year alongside a plan to reconnect electricity markets.

The BBC understands the draft agreements will also include some references to UK involvement in defence co-operation schemes, access to law enforcement databases and some reassurances over the EU's "Made in Europe" plans.

A food deal will have particular significance for Northern Ireland, radically reducing the impact of the post-Brexit Irish Sea border.

Northern Ireland's Brexit deal kept it inside the EU's single market for goods. That means food products from elsewhere in the UK require paperwork and some physical checks when arriving at NI's ports. A deal will ultimately end those requirements.

While the food trade plans have been backed by supermarkets and farmers representatives, some pro-Brexit campaigners have criticised the reapplication of EU rules. The post-Brexit trade deal signed by Boris Johnson's government prioritised regulatory freedoms for the UK over avoiding new regulatory red tape, checks and delays.

But William Bain, the British Chamber of Commerce's (BCC) head of trade policy, told the BBC's Today programme that business overall thought it was a "price is worth paying" to follow common rules, as companies would benefit from "lower costs, reduced delays [and] increased market access".

At the moment, he said, the requirement for Export Health Certificates can add thousands of pounds of costs to each truck carrying goods, in addition "to the delays that people find at the ports or at the borders".

"So we get lower exports because of the delays and the massive increases in costs that have come into the supply chain."

The BCC has released a new analysis of its members' views suggesting options for a closer relationship.

The business lobby group says the current trade relationship is "failing to deliver", leaving firms "struggling with a thicket of Brexit red tape" nearly six years after the UK's exit from the bloc.

#131. CVS to split off drugstore business in major breakup of healthcare giant; shares fall 10%

CVS Health plans to split into two separate public companies by the first half 2028 in what would be one of the largest corporate breakups in history, the company announced Wednesday.

The move would reverse the company's massive $78 billion acquisition of its insurer, Aetna, and dismantle the vertically integrated healthcare conglomerate it created in 2018. The plan would specifically separate CVS' roughly 9,000 retail pharmacies from its major insurance business and pharmacy benefit manager, Caremark, one of the nation's three largest PBMs.

The decision follows a "comprehensive strategic review" by the company's board of directors, according to a news release. Shares of CVS closed more than 12% lower on Wednesday.

It's a stunning reversal for the healthcare powerhouse, which has spent the last two decades working to turn itself into a one-stop health destination. The move also comes as CVS' aggressive turnaround plan under CEO David Joyner shows substantial progress, with the company pursuing $2 billion in cost cuts, store closures, leadership shake-ups and efforts to rein in Medicare Advantage costs.

But Joyner told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" on Wednesday that the decision to split is "out of a position of strength," pointing to recovery in Aetna's Medicare business and solid performance in the retail pharmacy.

"So it's all about the strength of the business that affords us the opportunity to make what we think are the right strategic decisions for the future of this company," he later added.

CVS shares have jumped roughly 70% since Joyner took the helm in October 2024, bringing the company's market value to $117 billion.

The two new companies would both be publicly traded.

The insurance and PBM company will retain the CVS Health name and be run by Joyner, while the other company will be called CVS Pharmacy and be led by Prem Shah, who is currently group president of CVS Health. Additional leadership positions and board members will be announced at a later date, CVS said.

Existing CVS shareholders will own stock in both companies after the split. The Financial Times on Tuesday first reported the breakup plans.

The company's pharmacy business raked in $37.66 billion in 2025 sales, while its insurance unit booked $36.29 billion in revenue and the healthcare services segment generated $51.24 billion.

CVS on Wednesday also posted third-quarter earnings and revenue that topped Wall Street expectations. Total sales climbed to $108.40 billion, from $102.87 billion in the prior-year period.

Sales were boosted by year-over-year growth in the company's healthcare benefits and health services segments. Meanwhile, revenue in its pharmacy services business slid from the previous year.

On Wednesday, CVS said it will lump its insurer and PBM with its primary care business Oak Street Health, home visit provider Signify Health and the company's specialty drug business.

CVS also said the split will allow the two companies to experience a "focused strategic agenda" and greater ability to "innovate, adopt technology, increase efficiency," among other benefits.

It's not the first time CVS has weighed splitting the company. In October 2024, CVS engaged advisors in a strategic review of its business and considered breaking up its retail pharmacy and insurance units.

But Joyner told CNBC that the company was "in a turnaround" at that time without the flexibility, cash position or business performance to execute a split.

In an interview, Leerink Partners analyst Michael Cherny said the split shows "the stability of the pharmacy," which has won market share from competitors and is seeing gross margins stabilize thanks to CostVantage, a new pharmacy reimbursement model introduced by CVS last year that the company bills as more transparent.

"This business is a much different business from the one that's been considered three years ago, five years ago, 10 years ago, where there had been a litany of headwinds that the pharmacy just couldn't overcome," he told CNBC on Wednesday.

One potential concern for investors is whether the retail pharmacy will lose its competitive advantage of being directly linked with a PBM, which negotiates drug prices and manages prescription drug benefits on behalf of insurers and employers.

But Joyner told analysts Wednesday that the synergy between the two businesses "are going to be preserved in commercial agreements between the two organizations," adding that "we don't need the integration of an operating structure."

Cherny said the rationale for the split could also extend to the state legislative scrutiny over the company's level of vertical integration.

The potential breakup also comes as pharmacy benefit managers face mounting bipartisan scrutiny in Washington, D.C., and across states. Republicans in Tennessee and Arkansas have passed laws that would ban co-ownership of PBMs and pharmacies. Those have been challenged in court by CVS and other companies.

PBMs have been criticized for opaque pricing practices, their growing market power and potential conflicts of interest when they are owned by companies that also operate insurers and pharmacies.

#129. What Republicans' huge loss means for Trump

Democrats just put President Donald Trump in a bind.

The party is projected to win commanding majorities in the House and Senate after thrashing Republicans in key races up and down the ballot across the country. The blue tsunami will knock Washington on its head and crush the Republican congressional bulwark that both supported and shielded Trump through the first two years of his term.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The midterm result snuffs out any chance Trump had of passing partisan legislation in the second half of his presidency. And many of the interests he has been able to pursue without Congress' input — including tariffs, the Iran war and an array of Washington-area construction projects — are sure to face sudden pushback from newly empowered Democratic majorities.

The paradigm shift hardly leaves Trump helpless, however. As president at a time of executive power expansion, Trump wields singular authorities that could stymie Democrats' efforts to pursue their own agendas.

But with his grip on the Capitol shriveling and his approval ratings scraping record lows in numerous polls, it's unclear how much support he'll be able to muster to fight the coming political battles.

Even before Election Day, analysts and pundits had contemplated a Democratic rout so decisive that it would force a reckoning for the GOP, and potentially even pose an existential threat to Trump's "Make America Great Again" movement.

Marc Short, chairman of Advancing American Freedom and onetime chief of staff to former Vice President Mike Pence, told CNBC on Tuesday afternoon that the GOP has moved away from its conservative foundations.

"We need a refresh right now," Short said in an interview. "We've lost our way."

Trump has been vague about what he hopes to achieve with the rest of his term. But he has shared a vision of how he might coexist with a Democratic majority, and it involves heavy use of the veto pen.

"It's one of the stronger powers that a president has," Trump said in a Fox News interview overnight. "They'll send over things that are insane, and I'll veto them."

"And after a couple of months of vetoes, they'll call and they'll say, 'How do we get this stuff done?' And we'll work together," he said, adding, "I have a lot of friends that are Democrats."

Trump has previously stated his willingness to veto reams of legislation that Democrats send across his desk. In a New York Post podcast interview released Monday, Trump said that if Republicans don't win, "I veto everything."

Pressed to detail his plans for the next two years, Trump touted his record and said, "The most important thing is to keep it going. It's going to get better and better and better, unless people come in that hate our country."

If that happens, "I guess I'll become Mr. Veto," Trump said.

He may soon get his chance: House Democrats, poised to be led by current Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries of New York, are planning to pass a flurry of bills as soon as they take the reins next January, CNBC has learned.

The legislative push, first reported by The New York Times, aims to show a united Democratic caucus taking quick action on the issues they were elected to solve, especially oppressively high costs on everything from gas to groceries.

In their first 100 hours after being sworn in, House Democrats plan to introduce bills reinstating the enhanced health insurance subsidies that expired under the GOP-held Congress, and reversing the Medicaid cuts Trump signed into law earlier this year.

Democrats also plan to push forward a bill that would undo Trump's sweeping tariffs and reassert Congress' role in setting import taxes. And they will again vote on legislation to end the war with Iran, or seek congressional authorization to keep it going.

But without Trump's approval, those bills may end up as little more than symbolic gestures, as Democrats will lack the two-thirds majorities in both chambers that are required to override a presidential veto without GOP help.

And there's no guarantee the bills will even make it out of Congress: Despite their landslide victories, Senate Democrats will still end up shy of the 60 votes required to bypass the chamber's filibuster rules for most legislation, giving Republicans key leverage in the minority.

Democrats also could feasibly benefit from Trump's vetoes in the long term. Forcing the president to shoot down numerous popular bills could give them fodder in the 2028 election.

It's far from clear whether Trump believes he can find compromise with Democrats on any of their initial legislative priorities.

But any potential for a working relationship with the opposition could be poisoned early on, as Democrats in both chambers are aggressively signaling their plans to launch politically charged investigations against Trump, his political and corporate allies and even his family members.

Winning control of House committees gives Democrats the ability to issue subpoenas under the current rules.

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., who is likely to chair the House Oversight Committee in the next Congress, said his panel will target the Trump family's business dealings, corporate corruption and alleged abuses by federal immigration agencies. He also plans to return focus to the Trump administration's handling of files related to notorious sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, Garcia summed up his plans in a two-word statement: "Lawyer up."

It's not just Garcia. Other Democrats have already demanded records from the Trump administration on a range of issues. Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., for instance, last week accused Trump of using tariff exemptions to cut "sweetheart deals" for industries whose leaders have plied the president with gifts.

Senate Democrats have sent similar signals.

"They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we'll be printing," Sen. Ed Markey, D-Mass., said in an X post late Tuesday in response to a post, appearing to refer to tech executives.

The investigations are widely expected to consume much of the rest of Trump's term.

Trump has ample experience being targeted by Democrats, who impeached him twice in the House during his first presidential term.

But with his base of support appearing to erode inside and outside of Washington, Trump may have to face his political foes with a smaller arsenal than he's used to.

"I'm not expecting Republicans to revolt" against Trump following the midterm losses, AAF's Short told CNBC. "But I'm expecting some of them to quietly distance themselves."

#128. Poundland owner in crunch talks as retailer's collapse looms

Business Secretary Jonathan Reynolds has met the US-based owners of Poundland as the government hopes to save the retailer from collapse.

The discount chain is on the brink of insolvency and Gordon Brothers, which bought it for Â£1 last June, has rejected a takeover bid which could have saved the 12,000 jobs currently at risk.

A meeting was due to be held on Thursday but has been brought forward as the scramble to secure a rescue deal for the firm intensifies.

Gordon Brothers has previously said Poundland faces multiple economic challenges from rising inflation, increasing staff costs, and "severe levels of shoplifting".

Both Reynolds and Prime Minister Andy Burnham have questioned why a deal for the future of the business has not been made.

On Wednesday, a source close to business secretary said Reynolds "has been firm that Gordon Brothers must do everything in their power to protect jobs and provide certainty for workers".

"He is pleased to have secured this commitment from Gordon Brothers in the meeting and will continue to hold the company to account and closely monitor developments," the source added.

It is understood that Gordon Brothers made a commitment in the meeting to prioritise bids that will protect workers and units.

However, it is still unclear whether the US investment firm will keep the business together or break it up.

Meanwhile, MPs say they have called in Poundland's owners for questioning on Wednesday 11 November to explain their role in the discount retailer's looming insolvency.

Liam Byrne, chair of Parliament's business committee, said: "Every report suggests Poundland is a viable, profitable business with a future ahead of it and without good reason to let it crash and burn - and then to break it up and sell it off piece by piece.

"We are asking Gordon Brothers to appear before Parliament to explain themselves and tell us just what it is that's preventing them from accepting the fully-funded deal on the table to save more than 12,000 jobs."

Business Minister Kate Dearden and union representatives met Poundland's managing director Barry Williams on Wednesday.

Mr Williams told the BBC that Poundland is profitable and sales were growing.

"We're back in profit and it has a bright future ahead of it if it's allowed to deliver that," he said.

Poundland has filed a notice of intention to appoint administrators, which protects it from its creditors until next Thursday.

Adam Turner: We would lose "a few million" if Poundland went under

Asked what his message for Poundland's owner was, Burnham said Gordon Brothers should "focus on the people in your employment" and "come up with a practical solution that saves jobs and saves those stores".

"There is a solution to be found here, sit down with us and let's find it," he added.

The meeting comes after a major Poundland supplier has said it will lose millions of pounds if the retailer goes bust.

Adam Turner, sales director at Bradford-based Highcrest Retail Brands, said Poundland was one of its biggest customers and its collapse would cost it "a few million" in lost sales.

The family owned business mainly sells confectionery to Poundland and turns over about Â£50m.

Turner said the firm becoming insolvent would leave a substantial gap in its balance sheet next year.

Highcrest was a "longstanding business that can stomach this", Turner said. But he warned other medium and small suppliers to the retailer have told him that "if Poundland goes under, so do they".

This echoes a warning from Poundland founder Steve Smith on Monday that two or three thousand suppliers to the firm, which only work for Poundland, would be directly affected by its collapse.

Not only would Poundland's 12,000 staff be affected in that case, but potentially up to 30,000 overall, he told the BBC on Wednesday.

He said he was "amazed" Poundland was being put into administration, pointing to the profitability of the company.

"Also, there's two offers on the table from some really good people," he added.

"All I'm asking is for Gordon Brothers to see if they can put a deal together."

Gordon Brothers said Walsall-based Poundland is "still not cash generative at a sustainable level" because of the economic issues it faces.

If Poundland's stores are sold to new buyers, some of the employees may get new jobs on the same premises.

However, it is understood that buyers are only interested in about half the stores at present â meaning roughly 6,000 employees would not be re-employed.

Poundland's founder offers to invest in firm

If Poundland goes under, suppliers will be getting "pennies in the pound" back from the administration, he said, which is "very upsetting" â although he still hopes a deal can be done so Poundland can remain solvent.

Poundland owners Gordon Brothers will be a secured creditor, which means they are first in line to get their money back, and suppliers "who have done nothing wrong" will take the financial hit.

"It's sad. It's a nightmare for us all to be honest with you. It's not just the suppliers, it's the staff at Poundland, some of these guys have been there for many years.

"It's from the warehouse staff all the way to the shop staff to everybody within head office. It's a massive ripple effect through the supply chain," he added.

Turner said he had no knowledge of whether Gordon Brothers had deliberately timed the process of Poundland going into administration so it could take advantage of having high stock levels, which it can then sell.

One of the ways Gordon Brothers makes money is through stock liquidations â selling off stock from collapsed businesses.

One insider familiar with the situation told the BBC that the timing of Poundland facing collapse was "odd" â most retailers don't fall over in the run-up to Christmas.

Dearden has said the government doesn't want to interfere with "current ongoing commercial negotiations", but that ministers want "clarity" on the plans for the retailer.

"It's absolutely legitimate for this government to ask the business if they're doing everything they can to protect employees and suppliers up and down the country," she added.

Dearden told MPs that the government would be ready to help the company's employees if Poundland collapses.

The head of Gordon Brothers' European operations reportedly told the government that the firm stood by its stewardship and had given Poundland Â£85m in additional financing since its purchase in 2025.

The chancellor has made welcome changes to tax-free saving and investing but could go further still

#126. New Flutter CEO bets on FanDuel promotions to win customers as profits suffer

Flutter's new CEO Dan Taylor is counting on better products and more generous customer rewards to help convert FanDuel's improving engagement into stronger profits.

Taylor's been at the helm for just five weeks and issued his first earnings report as CEO on Wednesday, with third-quarter revenue and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization missing Wall Street's expectations.

The company's stock is down more than 60% year to date.

Yet Taylor says the company is now on the right track.

"We made some changes to the product," Taylor told CNBC in an interview. "Fundamentally, the way you win this business is by delighting customers, delivering the very best experiences and delivering to them in the way that they want, with the right overall proposition."

FanDuel also launched a nationwide rewards program in September that Taylor said has "resonated really, really well."

The early customer numbers are improving. Average monthly players on FanDuel's U.S. sportsbook rose 9% in the third quarter, while bet counts increased 15%, Taylor said.

Flutter's earnings release showed total U.S. average monthly players up 12%, including 24% growth in iGaming. Sportsbook handle, or the total amount wagered, rose 13%, but sportsbook revenue grew just 4% as promotional spending increased.

Taylor said the company plans to keep investing in customer experience. He said Flutter sees room to improve its structural margins over time by changing the mix of products customers use.

Flutter has increased its media spend to build FanDuel Predicts, its predictions platform to rival Kalshi and Polymarket, but the cost of sales as a percent of revenue is forecast to be 58% for the full-year 2026 and into 2027.

Taylor said there's fierce industry competition for customers but even still, FanDuel trading volume has skyrocketed 271% since July.

The FanDuel Predicts app added 140,000 new customers in October, Taylor said.

Taylor also defended the long-term opportunity in the U.S., pointing out about half the country still does not have access to regulated sports betting and 90% lacks regulated online gaming.

He argued that expanding legal markets is not a partisan issue because regulated betting offers consumer protections and gives tax revenue to states.

For the third quarter, Flutter reported an adjusted loss of 18 cents per share, compared with analysts' expectations for a loss of 28 cents, according to LSEG. Revenue rose 2% to $3.87 billion, below the roughly $4 billion LSEG consensus, while adjusted EBITDA fell roughly 25% year over year to $360 million, also below expectations.

The company posted a net loss of $1.15 billion, including an $873 million non-cash impairment tied primarily to Brazil, which banned sports betting and online gaming in September.

Taylor said the company was disappointed by Brazil's provisional measure, but took the impairment to "set ourselves up to win long-term."

The complications in Brazil and unfavorable sports results in the U.S. and internationally led Flutter to cut its full-year revenue midpoint by $300 million to $17.61 billion and lower its 2026 adjusted EBITDA outlook by $160 million to nearly $2.5 billion.

In the meantime, capital returns are on hold: Taylor said Flutter needs to bring leverage back into its target range of 2 to 2.5 times over roughly the next 24 months before it can consider buybacks again. For now, he said, the priority is organic growth and paying down debt.

— CNBC's Mallory Leonard contributed to this report.

#125. Analysis: The Democrats have one chance to extract concessions from Trump. Markets will hate it

The electoral sweep by Democrats on Tuesday night injects a new, unstable dynamic into the single biggest political risk to financial markets in the coming year: the debt limit. Unless a defeated Republican Party makes a swift and unlikely show of unity to take the issue off the table in its last few weeks in power, the protracted fight that will unfold is set to be nastier than ever, adding volatility to an already shaky bond market.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

Past episodes of brinkmanship around the need to raise the government's borrowing limit have involved Republicans in Congress demanding budget cuts from a Democratic president as the price of their vote. This time it will almost certainly be the left seeking concessions from the right.

The debt limit is the definition of a must-pass bill, even more than issues like funding the government and the defense budget. That makes it potentially the one real chance the new Democratic majority in Congress has to extract concessions from a president who is already pledging to veto their agenda. Markets will hate the entire process, even if — as many expect — it ends in compromise.

Because it is Democrats who hold the leverage, unlike past episodes, the ultimate agreement is more likely to increase government spending than lower it. With high bond yields weighing on the economy, and Americans expecting action from a newly empowered Democratic opposition, the fight could be particularly damaging.

Many in the markets are stuck on the outdated idea that Democrats are less assertive than Republicans, said Aniket Shah, head of the Washington policy team at the investment bank Jefferies.

"They have this sort of mental image of Biden-Harris 2024," said Shah. "I don't think they realize how aggressively Democrats plan to take 2027."

The debt ceiling is a quirk of the congressional budgeting process. It stops the government from borrowing funds that Congress has already ordered federal agencies to spend. And because Congress has run deficits for decades — this year at $1.99 trillion — the pile of debt periodically bumps into the debt ceiling.

Congress last set the limit in 2025, when it gave the Treasury Department the authority to borrow $41.1 trillion. The Treasury is likely to hit its maximum borrowing capacity by midsummer, according to the Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonprofit that tracks the issue.

The Treasury can use budgeting techniques to extend the so-called X-date, when the U.S. would no longer be able to meet all its obligations, to sometime in the fourth quarter of 2027, according to Wall Street estimates.

A few technical hiccups aside, the U.S. has never meaningfully breached the debt limit. The results of the U.S. missing payments would be chaotic as damage ripples out across the financial system into the global economy. U.S. default would immediately call into question trillions in Treasury securities that are the lifeblood of the global financial system. Damaging them could send the U.S. and global economies into an economic tailspin with no obvious means of recovery.

Because the stakes are so high, Shah and many others on Wall Street have come to believe that the episode will produce volatility but will ultimately be resolved in compromise.

Fights under former Presidents Barack Obama and Joe Biden saw Republican congressional majorities use the issue to ram through spending reductions. (The 2025 raise was less contentious, because Republicans held Congress and the presidency. It was passed along party lines.)

Still, the risk-taking in those episodes contributed to ratings firms' decisions to downgrade U.S. debt — and could again.

President Donald Trump has called for Republicans to resolve the issue in the next few weeks, before the new Democratic-majority Congress is seated.

That isn't likely. The 2025 debt limit increase only passed because it was tied to a package of tax cuts and other policy changes. Republican debt hawks complained but held their noses.

Republicans may attempt to raise the limit in the coming lame-duck session under a budgeting procedure called reconciliation, which would allow them to move ahead without Democrats' votes. But success would require unity among a fractured, fleeting Republican majority that also faces several other more pressing priorities.

The most likely path toward raising the debt limit is the incoming Democratic majority negotiating another temporary fix with Trump. But they aren't going to make it easy. The president has occasionally made noises about making deals, but the political incentives ahead of the 2028 presidential election are for both sides to dig in.

"[Democrats] are going to want to embarrass the White House, and they're going to want to get leverage," Shah said. Democrats will use that to push to reverse cuts to Medicaid spending passed in 2025 and to try to restore healthcare spending, he said.

Democrats could also seek to tie the debt limit to restraining signature Trump policies, such as limiting the war with Iran or rolling back tariffs, analysts at Goldman Sachs said in a research note Wednesday.

Another potential compromise being considered in some corners of Washington is for Democrats to tie an increase in the corporate tax rate to a debt limit raise, said George Pollack, senior U.S. analyst with Signum Global Advisors. Trump has shown he can be surprisingly flexible on similar issues, Pollack noted.

That is what will make this debt limit fight different. In the past, Republicans used it to try to win spending reductions they couldn't get through regular budget negotiations. Now Democrats will broaden it out into an all-purpose hostage. That effort is far more likely to raise overall government spending than cut it. "There's a very weird irony here," said Robert Sockin, chief U.S. economist at PGIM, an asset manager.

The path to compromise will likely be rocky. Sockin believes it may turn out like the 2025 government shutdown. That was the longest government-wide shutdown in history, even though Democrats weren't in control of either chamber of Congress. This time they will have even more power.

If the fight extends beyond the X-date, missing debt payments could do serious damage to the financial system.

"It's not our base case, but we think the risk is higher than ever that you could get a technical default from the government," Sockin said. That would see the U.S. miss a payment, even if the government eventually makes it up.

That would still hurt. "You'll get more downgrades to the U.S. sovereign, and those might bite more this time," Sockin said. Yields on longer-term Treasurys would rise to account for added uncertainty.

Crossing the red line could prompt a "very negative reaction in equity markets," he said.

The coming months will show just how close Democrats are willing to get to that line.

#124. Elon Musk's millions failed to deliver Republican wins in fierce midterms

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Watch: Key moments from the US midterm election results

Elon Musk spent almost $450m (Â£334m) â more than anyone ever has in an election cycle â to back Republican political candidates in the US midterm races. It was not enough to keep Democrats from a resounding victory.

Among the most striking wins went to Democrat James Talarico, who beat Republican Ken Paxton for a US Senate seat in Texas. Musk spent at least $45m in support of Paxton, according to a BBC analysis of campaign finance data.

Other races where Musk spent millions included Arizona, Michigan, Wisconsin and Minnesota. His spending continued into Monday, records show.

By Tuesday night, Musk seemed to admit defeat, joking on X that his "election-interfering space lasers didn't work."

The loss this week of so many Republican seats may have significant implications for Musk and his businesses.

SpaceX has gained an estimated, external $10bn in new government contracts since Trump was elected to his second non-consecutive term. Several democratic lawmakers have said the company's extensive and growing role in government and national security should be investigated, external.

SpaceX's future development of massive data centres is also facing new potential difficulties - as are the data centre developments of other tech companies.

In Georgia for instance, where SpaceX has data centre operations, the just elected Democratic governor ran in-part on limiting the rapid rise of the massive buildings, as well as the huge tax breaks tech companies usually get to build them.

A number of other politicians ran on limiting data centres and dozens of state measures to limit their development passed on Tuesday, external.

Data centres are sprawling buildings that house stacks of computing hardware. Tech and artificial intelligence (AI) companies use them to run online platforms such as ChatGPT, Claude, and Musk's X and his AI company xAI.

Musk posted to X on Tuesday that his previously floated idea to somehow build data centres in space was "looking pretty appealing right now."

Five reasons why Republicans lost so badly

Democrats won Congress in a blue wave. Now comes the hard part.

Tech companies involved in AI may be in for a tougher government scrutiny and potential regulation with Democrats in power, though any legislation would need Trump's approval.

James Flintoft, head of investment solutions at AJ Bell, pointed to Musk's unsuccessful spending on Republican candidates and the scale of wins by Democrats as bringing in a "period of uncertainty for investors as they assess whether tech companies are set to face greater regulation."

Senator Ed Markey on Wednesday responded on X to a person asking if major tech industry executives would be the target of investigations, saying: "They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we'll be printing."

Tech stocks were broadly up on Wednesday morning. Yet, Musk's Tesla and SpaceX were down by 4.9% and 3.4% respectively.

By Wednesday, Musk had stopped posting to X about politics and was instead posting in praise of GrokBot, an AI chatbot produced by xAI. It was a stark contrast to the 2025 election, when Musk would post to X sometimes hundreds of times in a day about politics.

He also unpinned a post that had been saved to the top of his X profile since last week that explained his "concern" with Democratic politicians winning more midterm races.

Those concerns included the idea that the US could welcome more immigrants, make Washington DC and Puerto Rico individual states, and expand the number of justices on the Supreme Court.

Another post telling his 241 million X followers to "Vote red today!" appeared on Wednesday to have been deleted from Musk's profile.

The BBC has contacted SpaceX for comment about Musk's spending and posts.

While Musk spent millions of dollars backing Republican candidates in multiple US states, a lot of his political focus seemed to be on Texas.

In recent years, Musk made the state the corporate headquarters of Tesla and SpaceX, both of which still have large operations in California where they were founded.

"There is something deeply wrong with Talarico", Musk posted to X in October.

Talarico posted in response: "There is something deeply wrong with a political system that allows one trillionaire to buy his own personal US Senator."

Not all billionaires in the US shared Musk's preference for Republicans.

Reid Hoffman, a tech billionaire and the founder of LinkedIn who is a major donor to Democrats, hailed the results of Tuesday's elections as an event that "blew the doors off our expectations."

"These elections were an overwhelming wave against Trump and his cronies," Hoffman wrote on X and on LinkedIn. "While people like Elon are spending outrageous amounts to keep authoritarian clowns in power, those of us who believe in the ideals of America cannot yield."

#121. CVS Health plans to split itself up. Here's why a breakup could make sense now

CVS Health plans to break itself up — and this time, the bet looks a lot more viable.

The healthcare conglomerate on Wednesday announced plans to split into two publicly traded companies by the first half of 2028. CVS would separate its pharmacy business – including roughly 9,000 retail locations – from its major insurance business and pharmacy benefit manager, Caremark.

The move comes more than two years after CVS considered a breakup but ultimately walked away from the idea. At the time, analysts questioned whether the retail pharmacy business could survive on its own.

“Back then, the idea of retail standing alone was unthinkable,” Brian Tanquilut, senior equity research analyst at Jefferies, told CNBC.

But the economics of the businesses have changed: CVS's retail pharmacy business has become more consistently profitable, while its insurer, Aetna, has improved its performance despite persistently high medical costs in Medicare Advantage plans. That could make it easier for the company to separate the units — and for investors to recognize the value of each on its own, rather than valuing them together as one sprawling healthcare conglomerate.

“For us, it's showing the stability of the pharmacy,” Michael Cherny, senior research analyst at Leerink Partners, said in an interview. “This business is a much different business from the one that's been considered three years ago, five years ago, 10 years ago, where there had been a litany of headwinds that the pharmacy just couldn't overcome.”

CVS CEO David Joyner told CNBC’s "Squawk on the Street" on Wednesday that back in 2024, the company was "in a turnaround" without the flexibility, cash or business performance to execute a split. Now, CVS has posted eight consecutive quarters of beating Wall Street earnings estimates and has raised its guidance several times over the last two years, thanks to improvement across its PBM, pharmacy and insurer.

Since taking the helm in 2024, Joyner has pursued an aggressive turnaround plan that involves $2 billion in cost cuts, store closures, leadership shakeups and efforts to rein in Medicare Advantage costs.

The company also now expects to generate at least $15.5 billion in operating cash flow in 2026, a roughly 70% increase from $9.1 billion in 2024, and says it has reached its leverage target ahead of schedule.

"What's changed in two years is the fact that we now can look at this business and the assets that are assembled and really determine, without any distress or without anything that we have to do, what is the right strategic decision to make," Joyner told analysts on a call Wednesday, adding that the decision comes "out of strength."

While investors have largely cheered CVS's progress in recent years, Wall Street appeared more pessimistic about Wednesday's announcement, as the company's shares dropped about 12%.

CVS's retail business has benefited from changes in the competitive landscape, including the widespread retrenchment and store closures of Walgreens, Rite Aid and other pharmacy operators over the last few years. That’s created opportunities for CVS to gain market share, some analysts told CNBC.

CVS has also introduced changes to its pharmacy reimbursement model. Its CostVantage program is designed to replace the traditional process with a more transparent, cost-based model. Some analysts say that has helped the company better align reimbursement with the cost of dispensing prescriptions and contributed to steadier profits.

Those developments represent a meaningful shift for a business that has faced years of pressure from declining prescription reimbursement, store closures and competition from online pharmacies and other retailers.

That doesn't mean the retail pharmacy has become a high-growth business. CVS's retail pharmacy segment remains a relatively low-margin operation, with adjusted operating margins of roughly 4% to 5%. But analysts say it may now be stable and profitable enough to operate independently – even if investors value it at a discount to CVS's other businesses.

In a note Wednesday, RBC Capital Markets analyst Ben Hendrix said the lower valuation investors assign to the retail pharmacy unit has weighed on CVS's overall value and obscured the earnings strength of businesses like Aetna and Caremark.

The underlying growth of the insurance and PBM businesses is also stronger than that of retail pharmacy, Tanquilut said, after accounting for changes to Caremark's rebate arrangements.

Aetna in particular has gotten a better handle on medical costs by exiting unprofitable markets, expanding value-based care models, and tightening utilization management.

The breakup also comes as Caremark faces mounting scrutiny over PBM practices, including state-level legal and regulatory challenges involving the businesses and their relationships with pharmacies.

Republicans in Tennessee and Arkansas have passed laws that would ban any one company from owning both a pharmacy and a PBM — thought the Arkansas measure has been held up in court. Cherny noted that legal hurdles involving PBMs in other states have continued to pile up.

“Through what seems to be a never-ending litany of state lawsuits, it feels like it's been under more pressure,” Cherny said. “It's unclear if it just became, for lack of a better term, too tenuous to move forward with those two entities combined as one. I’m not sure.”

Even so, Joyner told the Wall Street Journal that the company wasn’t splitting up because of regulatory issues. CVS has said the separation follows a strategic review and is intended to give each business greater focus and flexibility.

The move also marks a significant departure from the integrated healthcare strategy CVS spent years building.

CVS spent roughly $70 billion to acquire Aetna in 2018 and argued that combining health insurance, pharmacy benefit management, retail pharmacies and health providers under one roof would help coordinate care and create a competitive advantage. CVS was still promoting that model as recently as its investor day last year.

The proposed split suggests the company now sees more value in separating at least some of those businesses – though it is not abandoning vertical integration altogether.

Aetna and Caremark will remain together in the healthcare company, alongside CVS’s specialty pharmacy, its primary care clinic Oak Street Health and its home-visit provider Signify Health. The separate pharmacy company will include roughly 9,000 retail stores, mail pharmacy operations and MinuteClinic, a retail medical clinic located inside certain CVS locations.

Both sides are large enough that they can stand on their own, Cherny said. Caremark is one of the country's three largest PBMs, while CVS has one of the largest retail pharmacy footprints in the U.S.

But separating the company raises questions about how much of the commercial relationship between the businesses can survive.

The relationship has historically benefited both sides. As it stands, Caremark can use CVS's extensive retail footprint to offer members convenient access to prescriptions and steer them toward CVS locations through preferred pharmacy arrangements. Programs such as Maintenance Choice, for example, give eligible members the option to fill certain maintenance medications at CVS retail pharmacies or through mail order.

Those arrangements also helped direct prescription volume to CVS's stores, giving the retail pharmacy unit an important source of customers. Because the businesses shared a parent company, the financial benefits of that relationship ultimately went to CVS as a whole.

In a note, RBC's Hendrix also raised questions about how the split could impact Aetna's Medicare Advantage quality ratings, which determine whether health plans qualify for bonus payments from the government. Retail pharmacists have played a role in engaging Aetna members, and a separation could complicate that relationship.

But Joyner told analysts Wednesday that the synergy between the two businesses would be "preserved in commercial agreements between the two organizations," adding that "we don't need the integration of an operating structure."

Joyner said CVS will be working on those potential agreements over the next 15 to 18 months.

Preferred pharmacy networks are common across the industry, and separate ownership does not prevent Caremark from continuing to favor CVS pharmacies through negotiated arrangements, Cherny said. The difference is that under separate ownership, the profits from those arrangements would no longer accrue to a single company, he noted.

“The issue is not whether the companies can continue working together in some capacity, but whether any future contractual arrangements preserve the SAME competitive advantages and flexibility as today under common ownership,” Cherny said in an afternoon note on Wednesday.

Still, both businesses face challenges on their own.

Tanquilut cautioned that some of CVS's recent retail pharmacy gains have come as Walgreens and other competitors have closed stores, raising questions about whether the growth can be sustained. The business also remains exposed to consumer pressure and competition from online pharmacies.

Caremark, meanwhile, faces continued regulatory scrutiny and potential headwinds from changes to the federal 340B drug discount program, which requires drugmakers to offer discounted medications to eligible hospitals and other healthcare providers serving low-income and vulnerable patients.

CVS participates in the program by providing pharmacy services to those organizations, but tweaks to manufacturer data-sharing policies and potential reimbursement changes could weigh on that business in 2027, Tanquilut said.

Aetna, meanwhile, is still working through a multiyear plan to rebuild its margins, as it looks to improve the profitability of its Medicare Advantage business.

Ultimately, CVS is betting that the value of letting investors assess its businesses separately will outweigh the benefits of keeping them under one roof. The split will test whether each can thrive independently.

#120. Pound rises to its highest level since the Brexit referendum

The pound has risen to its highest overall level since the Brexit referendum over a decade ago.

Sterling has risen notably in recent weeks against the euro and the dollar, ahead of an expected rise in interest rates.

The most-watched measure of the value of the British currency against a basket of 18 of the world's main currencies this week reached its joint highest level since the June 2016 referendum, according to Bank of England figures.

The value reached 86.4 on an index where 100 equals the value in 2005. The Bank will officially confirm the calculation on Thursday.

The value of sterling fell significantly after the Brexit referendum in June 2016, and reached further lows during the Brexit process, the Covid pandemic and the 2022 mini-Budget.

Some traders said the UK was being seen in a different light given relative political stability, solid growth and the rapprochement with the European Union.

Sterling has responded strongly to Brexit developments and political uncertainty in recent years.

Jim O'Neill, an economist and former Treasury minister, said: "I'm not surprised. People were, and to a lesser extent, are, underestimating the improving momentum in the UK,"

A stronger pound can help rein in inflation, and increase buying power for consumers on imports and on holidays.

UK growth so far this year has outperformed all expectations, but there are signs that higher energy and fuel prices have now hit consumer confidence.

The Bank's nine member Monetary Policy Committee faces an important decision on interest rates on Thursday, with the first rise in three years widely expected.

On Wednesday sterling was up 0.28% against the dollar at $1.3434 and was flat against the euro at â¬1.1996 as of 23:01 GMT.
